Working with measurement-based computations on qudits
Piotr Mitosek, Miriam Backens

Abstract
Measurement-based quantum computing is a universal model of quantum computation in which successive product measurements of an entangled resource state drive the computation. The non-deterministic nature of measurements necessitates adaptivity to ensure an overall deterministic computation. Flow structures characterise cases in which such an adaptive correction procedure is possible. Recently, flow has been defined in a setting where the resource states are prime-dimensional qudit graph states rather than the usual qubit graph states. Yet, this qudit flow definition is more burdensome to work with than analogous definitions for qubits. Here, we give a simpler definition of qudit flow and consider various useful properties of this flow, drawing on results for the qubit case. In particular, we show how to focus qudit flow and argue that focused flow is canonical. We improve the previous algebraic formulation to capture focused flow and use it to obtain an 𝒪 ⁡ ( n 3 ) \mathcal{O}(n^{3}) flow-finding algorithm (where n n is the number of qudits), matching the best known complexity for qubit flows and improving on the previous 𝒪 ⁡ ( n 4 ) \mathcal{O}(n^{4}) result for qudits. Furthermore, we explore multiple flow-preserving transformations, thus opening a pathway to using flow for optimisation. These transformations include pivoting, removal and insertion of certain types of vertices, and reversibility of flow. Lastly, we propose an algorithmic approach to generating large qudit computations with flow, for testing or machine learning.



1
Introduction

In computer science, it is standard to encode information in bits, i.e. using binary values 0 or 1.
This has carried across to quantum computing, where most research focuses on qubits, aka ‘quantum bits’, that have two classical states (and superpositions thereof).
True qubit systems exist in nature,
nevertheless, many of the most promising candidates for physical qubits naturally have more than two basic states,
and there is increasing interest in quantum computation using
d
-dimensional systems, called qudits.

Much of the theoretical research about qudit quantum computation focuses on the quantum circuit model
[
20
,
23
,
43
]
.
This means a computation is driven entirely by unitary gates; state preparation and measurements can be restricted to the computational basis and the latter are needed only for reading out information at the end.
The qudit Clifford fragment for prime dimensions
d
is core to qudit quantum computing analogous to its role with respect to qubits
[
25
,
20
]
.
There are definitions of qudit stabiliser states and also qudit graph states
[
6
]
, which in dimension
d
correspond to
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graphs: where in the binary case, edges are either present or not, in an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph, each pair of vertices is associated with a weight
w\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
such that
w=0
corresponds to there being no edge between the two vertices.

Qudit graph states then permit the generalisation of the one-way model of measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC)
[
36
]
to prime-dimensional systems
[
33
,
10
,
8
,
37
]
.
In the one-way model, an entangled resource graph state is prepared at the beginning; this resource can be independent of the computation (as long as it is large enough).
The computation proceeds by successive adaptive single qudit measurements in different
measurement spaces
(called measurement planes in the qubit case).
The individual measurements change the underlying state and can thereby drive a computation, yet they are not generally deterministic.
This is why the ‘successive adaptive’ part matters: If the combination of underlying graph state and measurement spaces for each qudit (formalised as a ‘labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph’) is chosen well, then it is possible to modify later measurements depending on the outcomes of earlier measurements so as to make the overall computation deterministic.
Whether overall determinism is possible is characterised by a property called
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
1
1

1

This property was previously called
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
-flow, yet it actually relies on properties of a field, not just a ring. We change the notation and terminology to emphasise this.

[
8
]
, the qudit equivalent of ‘extended gflow’ on qubits
[
11
]
.
Yet the current definition of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is somewhat unwieldy in practice as one validity condition requires considering any totalisation of the partial order that is part of the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow specification.

In this work, we develop a different perspective on
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow on qudits, which adapts the ‘algebraic Pauli flow’ formulation
[
31
]
to qudits and is more straightforward to work with.
This then allows several results from qubit gflow to be reproduced for qudits.
In particular, we adapt the qubit gflow concept of ‘focusing’
[
1
]
to
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow and show that the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is equivalent to the existence of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Next, we directly generalise previous algebraic formulations of qubit flow
[
29
,
32
,
31
]
: given a labelled open graph, we show simple linear algebra conditions allowing verification of the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
We also show how to lift the definition of and core results about qubit ‘focused sets’
[
39
,
31
]
to qudit
focused vectors
– these structures classify ‘do nothing’ stabilizers, i.e. stabilizers that do not impact the correction procedure.
We use the new algebraic interpretation to devise new fast algorithms for finding
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, improving previous
\mathcal{O}(n^{4})
routine
[
8
]
to
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, i.e. the complexity matching our qubit result
[
31
]
.
While
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow only works for prime
d
, our algorithms solve the underlying linear algebra problem over any finite field
\mathbb{F}_{d}
, not necessarily a prime field.
Lastly, we adapt some flow-preserving rules previously known for qubit flows to also work for qudit flow.
In addition to the local complementation and local scaling rules that were already known for the qudit case, this includes pivoting
[
17
,
1
,
39
]
, reversibility
[
31
]
, and the removal or insertion of a family of measurements
[
17
,
1
,
27
,
3
]
.
We also show how these flow-preserving rules allow the generation of arbitrary labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, generalising a recent result of one of the authors in the qubit case
[
5
]
.

The generalisation from qubits to qudits comes with several intricacies as the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
(having characteristic 2) behaves quite differently than
\mathbb{F}_{d}
for any other prime
d
.
In general, working over qudits means there are many more possible measurement spaces, correction operators, and graph symmetries to be considered and new choices had to be made to define e.g. the concept of focusing or a useful pivot operation in the qudit context.

In Section
2
, we provide the necessary background.
Next, in Section
3
, we adapt the notion of ‘focusing’ to the qudit case and show that focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow exists whenever any
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow exists.
In Section
4
, we give an algebraic formulation of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
We use this formulation in Section
5
to derive a faster flow-finding algorithm.
In Section
6
, we consider
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving rewriting.
Lastly, in Section
7
, we give conclusions and discuss potential further work.
Various technical parts of the paper are included in the appendices.

2
Background

We work with finite fields
\mathbb{F}_{d}
of order
d=p^{k}
for prime
p
and a positive integer
k
.
Unless specified otherwise, we assume throughout that
k=1
and thus
d=p
is prime.
We use
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
for
\mathbb{F}_{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
We also write
*
for an arbitrary element of
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
, i.e. an arbitrary non-zero element of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
All matrices are always over the field
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
Let
K
be a matrix with row labels
\mathcal{R}
and column labels
\mathcal{C}
.
We refer to it as
\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}
matrix.
We write
K_{v,*}
where
v\in\mathcal{R}
for the
v
-labelled row of
K
and, similarly,
K_{*,u}
with
u\in\mathcal{C}
for the
u
-labelled column of
K
.
Given
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
, we write
K_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
for the
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
submatrix of
K
.

A comparison of these concepts and other notions throughout this paper to what is known for qubits (i.e. the case
d=2
) can be found in Appendix
A
.

Definition 2.1
.

An
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph

G=(V,E)
is a simple loop-free
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
-weighted graph.
We write
n:=|V|
for the number of vertices of
G
.
We associate the graph with its
adjacency matrix
: the
V\times V
matrix
G
, where
G_{u,v}
stands for the weight of edge
(u,v)
, or
0
if there is no such edge.

Definition 2.2
.

An open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O)
is a triple where
G
is an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, and
I,O\subseteq V
are sets of input and output vertices.
We write
\bar{I}:=V\setminus I
and
\bar{O}:=V\setminus O
for the sets of non-input and non-output vertices respectively.
Finally, we write
B:=V\setminus(I\cup O)
for the set of internal vertices (B for in-Between).

Definition 2.3
.

A labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
is a quadruple where
(G,I,O)
is an open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and
\lambda\colon\bar{O}\to\mathbb{F}_{d}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
is a
measurement labelling
such that for all
i\in I\setminus O
:
\lambda(i)=(0,*)
.

2.1
Graph states and the one-way model of measurement-based quantum computation

Like graphs in the qubit case,
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graphs can be used to define a class of quantum states on qudits called
graph states

[
6
, Section C]
.
These states are stabiliser states, meaning they are eigenstates with eigenvalue 1 of certain tensor products of the qudit Pauli matrices.

We first introduce some of the formalism of qudit stabiliser quantum mechanics.
Let
\{\left|k\right\rangle\}_{k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}
be the computational basis of a qudit, then the Pauli-
Z
and
X
matrices are defined as
Z\left|k\right\rangle=\omega^{k}\left|k\right\rangle
and
X\left|k\right\rangle=\left|k+1\right\rangle
, where
\omega=e^{\frac{2i\pi}{d}}
and the addition inside the ket is modulo
d
.
The qudit Hadamard operator performs the discrete Fourier transform of dimension
d
, it is defined as
H\left|k\right\rangle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\omega^{jk}\left|j\right\rangle
.
Unlike for qubits, the qudit Hadamard is not self inverse; instead it satisfies
H^{4}=I
.
It is straightforward to show that
Z^{d}=I=X^{d}
and the gates satisfy
ZX=\omega XZ
,
HZH^{-1}=X^{-1}
, as well as
HXH^{-1}=Z
.
The
Pauli group
on one qudit is generated by
Z
and
X
, its elements are of the form
\omega^{a}X^{b}Z^{c}
for some
a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
A tensor product of single-qudit Pauli group elements is called a
Pauli product
.
In the following, for any single-qudit unitary
U
whose eigenvalues are
\{\omega^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}
, we will use
\left|k:U\right\rangle
to denote the eigenstate of
U
with eigenvalue
\omega^{k}
.
In particular,
\left|k:Z\right\rangle=\left|k\right\rangle
and
\left|k:X\right\rangle=H^{-1}\left|k\right\rangle
for all
k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
We will also need an entangling two-qudit gate, the controlled-
Z
gate defined as
CZ\left|jk\right\rangle=\omega^{jk}\left|jk\right\rangle
.
As for qubits,
CZ
gates applied to any (even overlapping) pairs of qudits commute.

In the following, we use subscripts to denote the qudit (or qudits) to which a gate is applied, e.g.
CZ_{u,v}
acts on qudits
u
and
v
.

Definition 2.4
.

Suppose
G=(V,E)
is an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, then the associated
graph state
is given by
\left|G\right\rangle:=\prod_{u,w\in V}CZ_{u,w}^{G_{u,w}}\bigotimes_{v\in V}\left|0:X\right\rangle
.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
6
]
)
.

Suppose
G=(V,E)
is an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, then the stabiliser group for the graph state
\left|G\right\rangle
is generated by the Pauli products of the form
X_{v}\prod_{w\in V}Z_{w}^{G_{v,w}}
for each
v\in V
.

In the one-way model of MBQC, a computation is performed via successive adaptive single-qudit measurements on a resource (
\mathbb{F}_{d}
)-graph state.
Each measurement has a distinguished
desired outcome
, which is the one driving the computation in the desired direction.
While individual measurements are non-deterministic, by restricting measurements to specific well-behaved measurement spaces, it is possible to compensate for a specific measurement having resulted in an undesired outcome through changing later measurements.
In this way one can use measurements to perform a computation that is overall deterministic.
The following presentation of the one-way model on qudits follows
[
8
]
.

A projective measurement on a qudit consisting of measurement projectors
\{\pi_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}
can be identified with a unitary matrix
U=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\mu_{k}\pi_{k}
for some set of eigenvalues
\mu_{k}
.
We will assume that the desired outcome is associated with eigenvalue 1, i.e. we consider only unitary representations of measurements that satisfy
U\left|\psi\right\rangle=\left|\psi\right\rangle
for some
\left|\psi\right\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
called the
fixpoint
.

Each single-qudit Pauli
X^{a}Z^{b}
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
such that
a,b
are not both 0 determines a
measurement space
, which is denoted
\mathcal{M}(a,b)
and which groups together measurements with shared characteristics.
The measurement labelling
\lambda
of a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph (Definition
2.3
) refers exactly to these measurement spaces.
Then a measurement unitary
U
is in
\mathcal{M}(a,b)
if and only if it satisfies
X^{a}Z^{b}U=\omega UX^{a}Z^{b}
, implying the following useful properties.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
8
, Proposition 2]
)
.

If
U\in\mathcal{M}(a,b)
, then the spectrum of
U
is
\{\omega^{m}\mid m\in\mathbb{F}_{d}\}
, each eigenvalue has multiplicity 1, and
U
is special unitary.
Denoting
\left|0:U\right\rangle
the fixpoint of
U
, then
\left|m:U\right\rangle=(X^{a}Z^{b})^{-m}\left|0:U\right\rangle
is an eigenvector of
U
associated with eigenvalue
\omega^{m}
.

If the measurement
U
yields the desired outcome, then the projection applied to the resource state is
\left|0:U\right\rangle\!\left\langle 0:U\right|
.
If instead the measurement yields an undesired outcome, the projection is
\left|m:U\right\rangle\!\left\langle m:U\right|
for some
m\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
.
By the above proposition, the undesired projectors are linked to the desired projector via powers of
X^{a}Z^{b}
:
\left|0:U\right\rangle\!\left\langle 0:U\right|=(X^{a}Z^{b})^{m}\left|m:U\right\rangle\!\left\langle m:U\right|(X^{a}Z^{b})^{-m}
.

This is the basis for the adaptive procedure that can correct undesired measurement outcomes: Suppose the measurement on qudit
v
has resulted in the undesired outcome
m\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
.
If there is a stabiliser of the underlying graph that acts on
v
as
(X^{a}Z^{b})^{m}
and otherwise acts non-trivially only on qudits that have not yet been measured, then this stabiliser can be used to correct the measurement on
v
.
The Pauli operators of the correcting stabiliser that act on qudits other than
v
itself are called
by-products
.
The assumption that non-trivial by-products arise only on unmeasured qudits implies that they can later be incorporated into the measurement on that qudit.
Generalising from a single measurement to an entire computation, we need a partial order on the measurements that indicates which measurements happen before or after others, as well as a correcting stabiliser for each measured qubit; the partial order and the stabilisers need to be compatible to ensure that by-products affect only qudits that come later in the partial order.
Some care also needs to be taken around treating qudits that act as inputs for the entire computation, meaning they are not prepared in the state
\left|0:X\right\rangle
but arrive in some arbitrary state and are entangled into the graph; as well as qudits that are not measured but remain as outputs at the end.
These conditions were formalised in
[
8
]
, we will introduce them now.

2.2
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

We use
[
10
, Lemma 4.3]
as a definition of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow instead of the original version of the definition in
[
8
, Definition 9]
, changing the name to ‘
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow’ with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
to emphasise that the underlying algebraic structure needs to be a finite field.

Definition 2.7

(
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
[
10
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

A labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
if there exists a tuple
(C,\prec)
where
C\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{V\times V}
is called the
correction matrix
and
\prec
is a strict partial order on
V
, such that:

(CF)

\forall u\in\bar{O}.\lambda(u)=(C_{u,u},(GC)_{u,u})
,

(ZF)

C_{u,v}=0
whenever
u\in I
or
v\in O
, and

(OF)

when the columns and rows of
G
and
C
are ordered according to any totalisation of
\prec
, then
C
and
GC
are lower triangular.

The
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition implicitly specifies the Pauli product
\bigotimes_{u\in V}X^{C_{u,v}}Z^{(GC)_{u,v}}
for correcting the measurement on qudit
v
.
It is straightforward to check that this is indeed a stabiliser of the underlying graph state.
Indeed, the flow correction condition
(CF)
ensures that the Pauli by-products and the desired correction together form a stabiliser for each measured qudit.
The zeroes condition
(ZF)
states that inputs cannot correct other vertices and that outputs do not need a correction.
Finally, the order condition
(OF)
states that it is possible to correct measurements in a causal way.
Out of the three conditions, the third one is clearly the most complicated.
Not only is it the only condition in which the strict partial order
\prec
is involved, the condition also considerers any totalisation of this strict partial order.

A worked example presenting
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is given in Figure
1
.
The unweighted graph underlying this example is a modification of the graph in
[
1
, Example 2.43]
.
Verifying that the relevant matrices are lower triangular with respect to some totalisation is a mildly tedious step that requires reordering rows and columns.

Figure 1
:
Running example of a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
d=5
.
1(a)
: a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with one input
i
and two outputs
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
. The measurement labels of non-outputs are given near the names of the vertices. Edge weights are denoted by numbers near the middles of the edges.
1(b)
: the corresponding adjacency matrix. The dotted lines subdivide input, internal, and output vertices for improved readability but otherwise play no role.
1(c)
: the corresponding correction matrix. This matrix is lower triangular.

: the product of adjacency and correction matrices from
1(b)
and
1(c)
respectively. It is not lower triangular, however it becomes lower triangular when the vertices are ordered
i\prec a\prec d\prec b\prec o_{1}\prec o_{2}
. Under this ordering, the correction matrix from
1(c)
remains lower triangular, hence the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

3
Focusing
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

If a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, this
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is not generally unique.
For qubits, the notion of
focused gflow
(or its generalisation, focused Pauli flow) has been very useful: for example, focused flow is unique if there are equal numbers of inputs and outputs
[
29
,
1
,
39
]
, it can be easier to work with for flow-preserving rewriting
[
17
,
1
,
3
]
, and it enables a linear-algebraic formulation of the flow conditions, leading to more efficient flow-finding algorithms
[
29
,
31
]
.
In this context, ‘focusing’ means restricting the correction by-products that a qubit can receive, depending on its measurement label.
We now generalise this idea to qudit
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow. We use notation
\left(\lambda_{X}(v),\lambda_{Z}(v)\right):=\lambda(v)
.

Definition 3.1
.

An
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
on a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
is
focused
when:

(FX)

for all
v\in\bar{O}
such that
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, we have
(GC)_{v,u}=0
for any
u\in\bar{O}\setminus\{v\}
and

(FZ)

for all
v\in\bar{O}
such that
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, we have
C_{v,u}=0
for any
u\in\bar{O}\setminus\{v\}
.

For example, the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow from Figure
1
is focused.

The first condition
(FX)
(
F
for focused) ensures that
(0,*)
-measured vertices receive only by-products of the form
X^{a}
(with trivial
Z
component).
All other vertices receive only by-products of the form
Z^{b}
(with trivial
X
-component), as captured by condition
(FZ)
.
This corresponds to focusing conditions for the qubit case:
XY
-measured vertices (now generalised to
(0,*)
) can only get an
X
by-product, while
YZ
and
XZ
(now generalised to
(*,0)
and
(*,*)
respectively) can only get a
Z
by-product.

We show, that existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is equivalent to existence of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
The proof may be found in Appendix
B
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph which has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Then there exists a focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow on
(G,I,O,\lambda)
.

Later on, in Corollary
4.6
, we will explain that focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow can be viewed as canonical, in the sense that when
|I|=|O|
, then there exists a unique focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, up to extensions of the partial order.

4
Algebraic formulation of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

The definition of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow gives linear algebraic conditions that a correction procedure must satisfy to enable deterministic MBQC.
Yet, condition
(O)
is awkward as it asks for a specific property to hold for any totalisation of the partial order.
We now refine the algebraic formulation, tailoring it specifically towards focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
This loses some generality – the new definition can no longer express non-focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow – but we already proved that any labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow also has focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
The small restriction is therefore outweighed in many cases by the associated gains in simplicity and efficiency of avoiding the arbitrary totalisations of the partial order.

First, we reduce some redundancy.
In Definition
2.7
, the correction matrix
C
is a
V\times V
matrix, but the condition
(ZF)
forces
I
-labelled rows and
O
-labelled columns of
C
to be identically zero.
Thus, instead of considering the full
V\times V
matrix
C
, it suffices to instead consider its
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
submatrix.
Correspondingly, it suffices to consider a submatrix of the adjacency matrix; because we want to multiply the two submatrices, the shape of this second submatrix will be
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
:

Definition 4.1
.

Given an open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
graph
(G,I,O)
, the matrix
A_{(G,I,O)}:=G_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{I}}
is its
reduced adjacency matrix
.

The reduced adjacency matrix allows the following restatement of the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition, with a proof of equivalence of two definitions given in Appendix
C.1
.

Definition 4.2

(
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow)
.

A labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
, where
C\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{I}\times\bar{O}}
is called a
reduced correction matrix
and
\prec
is a strict partial order on
\bar{O}
such that:

(C)

\forall u\in\bar{O}\setminus I.\lambda(u)=(C_{u,u},(AC)_{u,u})
and
\forall u\in I\cap\bar{O}.\lambda_{Z}(u)=(AC)_{u,u}

(O)

when the columns and rows of
A
and
C
are ordered according to any totalisation of
\prec
, then
C
and
AC
are lower triangular.

The reduced adjacency matrix and reduced correction matrix of the running example from Figure
1
are given in Figures
2(a)
and
2(b)
, respectively.

For the new formulation, instead of working directly with the (reduced) adjacency matrix of the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, we will define two matrices (both of shape
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
) that encode the measurement labels as well as relevant properties of the adjacency matrix.
First, we introduce some relevant notation.
We write
\delta_{a,b}
for the Kronecker delta, i.e.
\delta_{a,b}=1
when
a=b
and
0
otherwise.
We write
\Id_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{C}}
for a
\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}
matrix where
(\Id_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{C}})_{u,v}=\delta_{u,v}
for all
u\in\mathcal{R}
and
v\in\mathcal{C}
.
When
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{C}
,
\Id_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{C}}
is the identity matrix.
When
|\mathcal{R}|=1
or
|\mathcal{C}|=1
, then
\Id_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{C}}
is a row or column vector, respectively, which has zeroes everywhere except for the at most one entry in
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{C}
.
We use
\support\left(\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
to denote the support of the vector
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
, i.e. if
\mathcal{R}
is the set of labels of the elements of
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
, then
\support\left(\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)=\{r\in\mathcal{R}\mid\vec{\mathscr{a}}_{r}\neq 0\}
.

Definition 4.3

(Flow-demand matrix)
.

Given a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
, the corresponding
flow-demand matrix

M
is a
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
matrix where for
v\in\bar{O},u\in\bar{I}
:

\displaystyle M_{v,u}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}A_{v,u}&\text{if}\ \lambda_{X}(v)=0\\
\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}\delta_{v,u}&\text{if}\ \lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0\end{cases}

The expressions in the definition above are valid, in the sense that the inverse of
\lambda_{Z}(v)
is used only when
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, in which case
\lambda_{Z}(v)\neq 0
as
\left(\lambda_{X}(v),\lambda_{Z}(v)\right)=\lambda(v)\neq(0,0)
by Definition
2.3
.
Moreover, the required inverses exists due to
\mathbb{F}_{d}
being a field.
Similarly, the inverse of
\lambda_{X}(v)
is only used when
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
.
Furthermore
A_{v,u}
exists in the reduced adjacency matrix as
v\in\bar{O}
and
u\in\bar{I}
.
See Figure
2(c)
for an example of the flow-demand matrix and Appendix
E
for pseudocode that constructs this matrix from a specification of the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph.

Definition 4.4

(Order-demand matrix)
.

Given a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
, the corresponding
order-demand matrix

N
is an
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
matrix where for
v\in\bar{O},u\in\bar{I}
:
N_{v,u}=\lambda_{X}(v)A_{v,u}-\lambda_{Z}(v)\delta_{v,u}
.

See Figure
2(d)
for the order-demand matrix of the running example, and Appendix
E
for pseudocode that generates the order-demand matrix from the specification of a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph.

We can now state the main result of this section:

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
is a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph; let
M
be its flow-demand matrix and
N
its order-demand matrix.
Then
\Gamma
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow if and only if there exists a matrix
C
(of shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
) such that
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
and
NC
forms a (weighted) directed acyclic graph.

Here, the focusing conditions
(FZ)
and
(FX)
are fully captured by the requirement that
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
.
The condition
(C)
is mostly, but not fully, captured by the requirement
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
.
Part of
(C)
is instead captured by the property that the main diagonal of the product
NC
is identically
0
, which follows from
NC
being a DAG.
The condition
(O)
is fully captured by
NC
being a DAG.
The proof of Theorem
4.5
is given in Appendix
C
.
See Figure
2
for the application of this result to the running example from Figure
1
.

When
|I|=|O|
, the flow-demand matrix is square and has at most one right inverse: the two-sided inverse. Therefore, there exists at most one focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow (up to refinements of the partial order). Thus the focused flow uniqueness result known for qubits
[
29
,
1
,
39
]
generalises:

Corollary 4.6
.

Suppose
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
is a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph where
|I|=|O|
, then all focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow s on
\Gamma
share the same reduced correction matrix.

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
pNiceArray (nicematrix)

pNiceArray (nicematrix)

†

pNiceArray (nicematrix)

†
†

pNiceArray (nicematrix)

i
a

d

(f)

Figure 2
:
The algebraic formulation of the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Figure
1
.
2(a)
: the reduced adjacency matrix which is obtained from the matrix in
1(b)
by removing output rows and input columns; the reduced correction matrix in
2(b)
is obtained from the matrix in
1(c)
by a transpose of this process.
2(c)
: the flow-demand matrix, which has the reduced correction matrix in
2(b)
as its right inverse.
2(d)
: the order-demand matrix.
2(e)
: the product of the order-demand matrix with the reduced correction matrix. This matrix corresponds to the relation
\triangleleft_{C}
in
2(f)
, which is a DAG. Hence, the reduced correction matrix results in focused flow.

4.1
Focused vectors

In the qubit case, the kernel of the flow-demand matrix encodes a structure called
focused sets

[
31
, Section 3.4]
.
These sets, originally defined by Simmons
[
39
, Definition 4.3]
, correspond to ‘do nothing’ stabilisers, i.e. stabilisers that do not help with correcting measurement by-products in any way.
We extend the notion of focused sets to higher dimensions and recover the relationship to the kernel of the flow-demand matrix.
As a corollary, since the kernel of a matrix forms a vector space and thus an Abelian group, we also recover the Simmons qubit result
[
39
, Lemma B.8]
.
Relevant proofs are included in Appendix
C.3
.

Definition 4.7

(Focused vector)
.

Given a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
, a column vector
\vec{\mathscr{a}}\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{I}}
is
focused over

S\subseteq{\bar{O}}
if:

(Fs1)

\forall w\in S\cap\support\left(\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right).\lambda_{X}(w)=0
and

(Fs2)

\forall w\in S\cap\support\left(A\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right).\lambda_{X}(w)\neq 0
.

A vector focused over
\bar{O}
is simply called
focused
. The set of all focused vectors for
\Gamma
is denoted
\mathfrak{F}_{\Gamma}
.

Focused vectors could also be treated as multisets with support contained in
\bar{I}
and multiplicities given by elements of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
We usually treat them as vectors, as that is most convenient for the proofs, but all interpretations are equivalent.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
M
be its flow-demand matrix.
Then
\mathfrak{F}_{\Gamma}\cong\ker M_{\Gamma}
.

For example, the kernel of the flow-demand matrix
M
from Figure
2(c)
is
\ker M=\vspan\left((0,0,0,1,3)^{T}\right)
and thus the only focused vectors of the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph in Figure
1(a)
are those of form
k(0,0,0,1,3)^{T}
for some
k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
Under the multiset interpretation, these consist of vertex
o_{1}
with multiplicity
k
and vertex
o_{2}
with multiplicity
3k
.

5
Flow-finding

With the new formulation of focused flow, we now generalise the flow-finding algorithm from
[
31
]
to qudits.
In our previous work
[
31
, Corollary 4.1]
, we presented algorithms for qubit flow which effectively solve
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding when
d=2
, in the process solving the following linear algebra problem over the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
only
2
2

2

Our previous work in
[
31
]
addresses the more general setting of Pauli flow, in which measurements in the eigenbasis of a Pauli operator can be accorded special treatment. However when Pauli measurements are not treated separately, Pauli flow collapses to gflow, the latter being exactly the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow where
d=2
.
:

\mathbf{Inverse+DAG}

Input:
Two matrices
M
and
N
of matching shape
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.

Output:
A
\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{A}
matrix
C
over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
such that
MC=\Id_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
NC
forms a directed acyclic
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph, or the message that no such
C
exists.

The structure of the qubit algorithms can be understood as follows: given a labelled open graph, construct the flow-demand and order-demand matrices.
Next, solve
\mathbf{Inverse+DAG}
.
The qubit flow-finding algorithms do not assume anything about the structure of
M
and
N
after their initial construction.
These algorithms can be adapted to solve
\mathbf{Inverse+DAG}
, and thus
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding, for any
d
such that
\mathbb{F}_{d}
forms a fields.
The parameter
d
does not have to be prime, but only a power of a prime.
We obtain:

Theorem 5.1
.

There exists a
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
algorithm that, given a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, outputs the corresponding
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, or gives a message that no
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow exists.

For explanation of how to incorporate the computational complexity of finite field arithmetic into the complexity analysis, see Appendix
D
.

In the qubit case, we split flow-finding into two cases depending on whether
|I|=|O|
, i.e. on whether the input to
\mathbf{Inverse+DAG}
is given by square matrices.
When
|I|=|O|
, the algorithm for the qubit case applies to qudits without any changes.
However, in the general case, the qubit algorithm must be adjusted.
We include the pseudocode for
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding in the general case in Appendix
E
together with an explanation of where it differs from the qubit case.
Rather than writing out what would be near identical proofs to those in
[
31
]
, we provide a working example of finding flow for the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Figure
1
, see Appendix
F
.

Speed-ups to matrix multiplication and inversion can be deployed when
|I|=|O|
, reducing the complexity from
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
to a possibly smaller exponent, see
[
31
, Section 4.3.1]
for details.
We also obtain a lower bound by linking
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding to the problems of matrix multiplication and matrix inversion over a finite field, see Appendix
G
.

6
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow–preserving rewriting

So far, we have worked with a fixed labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph in defining and finding
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, and relied only on the data contained in this labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph.
To implement a one-way computation, more information is needed, in particular it is necessary to specify a measurement operator (with its desired outcome) for each measured qudit.
Given such an expression for an entire computation in the one-way model, one immediate question is about alternative ways of implementing the same linear operation, which may be more efficient, better suited to a specific hardware connectivity, or have other desired properties.
Thus, in the qubit case, there is a thriving system of ‘flow-preserving rewrite rules’
[
17
,
1
,
39
,
27
,
28
,
3
]
which map some given one-way computation to a different computation implementing the same linear operation.
These rules can be used to optimise quantum computational resources in the circuit or one-way models according to different metrics
[
40
,
24
]
, or even to obfuscate quantum computations so they can be delegated into the cloud while preserving security properties
[
12
]
.

We will therefore now consider flow-preserving rewriting for
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

6.1
Local scaling, local complementation, and pivot

Some relevant rewrite rules were already defined and proved flow-preserving by Booth
[
10
, Section 5.1]
.
These rules are based on the two different types of operations on an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph which translate to local Clifford equivalences of the corresponding qudit graph states
[
6
, Section D]
.
The first of these operations is
local scaling
, which multiplies the weights of all edges incident on a chosen vertex
w
by some non-zero constant
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
and is usually denoted
G\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w
.
The second operation is
local complementation
, which is also parametrised by a constant
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
and changes the weights of all edges among the neighbours of the chosen vertex
w
in a more complicated way, the resulting graph is denoted
G\overset{\gamma}{\star}w
.
In both cases, the graph state
\left|G\right\rangle
corresponding to the initial graph can be transformed into the graph state corresponding to the final graph (
|G\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w\rangle
or
|G\overset{\gamma}{\star}w\rangle
, respectively) by applying a product of single-qudit Clifford operations.
As a result, both operations preserve the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, given certain updates to the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
[
10
, Propositions 5.5 and 5.8]
; formal statements of this are given in Appendix
H.1
.

A
pivot
, a sequence of three local complementations about the endpoints of an edge, gives rise to a flow-preserving transformation on qubits and it interacts more smoothly with focused gflow and focused Pauli flow than individual local complementations
[
3
, Lemma 5.3]
.
The pivot has been generalised to a family of pivot operations on qutrits
[
42
, Theorem 3.5]
and a qudit pivot-and-delete operation (where the endpoints of the edge are deleted after the pivot)
[
34
, Lemma 10]
.
We work out the effects of a pivot operation (without deletion) for general prime-dimensional qudits in Appendix
H.1
and summarise them here.

Proposition 6.1

(informal statement)
.

Let
G
be an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph, suppose
w,w^{\prime}\in V
satisfy
\epsilon:=G_{w,w^{\prime}}\neq 0
, and define
G\wedge ww^{\prime}
to be the graph after a pivot on the edge
ww^{\prime}
.
Let
F^{(\gamma)}:=\sum_{j,k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\omega^{\gamma jk}\left|j\right\rangle\!\left\langle k\right|
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
, then
\left|G\wedge ww^{\prime}\right\rangle=F^{(-\epsilon)}_{w}F^{(-\epsilon)}_{w^{\prime}}\prod_{v\in V}R_{v}^{2\epsilon^{-1}G_{v,w}G_{v,w^{\prime}}}\left|G\right\rangle
.

We also argue that the above pivot operation can be implemented using three successive local complementations and at least one local scaling (the latter is needed to get a pivot operation that is symmetric under interchange of
w
and
w^{\prime}
).
This decomposition immediately implies that a pivot preserves the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

6.2
Flow reversal

In the qubit case, for a labelled open graph with
|I|=|O|
, it is possible to reverse the direction of flow
[
29
,
31
]
by swapping inputs with outputs (and assigning
XY
measurement labels to the former outputs, i.e. the new inputs).
Moreover, there is a close relationship between the flow in one direction and the flow in the other direction: for the fragment consisting only of
XY
measurements, inputs and outputs, the correction matrix in the reverse direction is just the transpose of the original correction matrix, although this relationship is more complicated for the other measurement planes.
We lift this result to qudits.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph which satisfies
|I|=|O|
.
Then its
reverse labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
is
\Gamma^{\prime}=(G,O,I,\lambda^{\prime})
, where

\lambda^{\prime}(v)=(0,1)
if
v\in O
and
\lambda^{\prime}(v)=\lambda(v)
if
v\in B
.

Theorem 6.3

(
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow reversibility)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph which satisfies
|I|=|O|
.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the reverse of
\Gamma
.
Then
\Gamma
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow if and only if
\Gamma^{\prime}
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

The proof is in Appendix
H.3
; it is quite close to the qubit case, except that working over an arbitrary prime field requires extra care with subtraction (which over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
is equivalent to addition).

6.3
Z
-like removal and insertion

In a focused flow, certain kinds of measured vertices receive only by-products that are powers of
Z
, these vertices are called ‘
Z
-like’.

Z
-like measured qubits can be removed from one-way computations
[
17
,
1
,
39
]
and, under certain conditions, inserted into one-way computations
[
3
]
while preserving the existence of flow.
We lift these flow-preserving qubit removal and insertion results to qudits; the corresponding proofs are presented in Appendix
H.4
.

For any
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
G
and any vertex
v
in
G
, we denote by
G-v
the graph that results from removing
v
(and all its incident edges).

Definition 6.4
.

Let
(G,I,O)
be an open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
S\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{V}
be any vector.
Define the
open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph extended by
v
as the open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G^{\prime},I,O)
obtained by inserting a new vertex
v
into
(G,I,O)
with its connections determined by
S
, i.e.
G^{\prime}-v=G
and
G^{\prime}_{v,u}=S_{u}
for all
u\in V
.
Given
(G,I,O,\lambda)
and
\lambda^{\prime}(v)
, we define the
labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph extended by
v
with measurement label
\lambda^{\prime}(v)
as the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
, where
(G^{\prime},I,O)
is the open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph extended by
v
, and
\lambda^{\prime}(u)=\lambda(u)
for all
u\in\bar{O}
.

From now on, we add a prime symbol for all notation referring to the (labelled) open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph extended by
v
; this includes
G^{\prime}
,
\lambda^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime}
,
\bar{O}^{\prime}
, and
\bar{I}^{\prime}
.

Theorem 6.5

(
Z
-like deletion)
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}:=(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
be the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
extended by
v
, where
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)\neq 0
, and suppose
\Gamma^{\prime}
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Then
(G,I,O,\lambda)
also has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

For
Z
-like insertion, we restrict to the case
|I|=|O|
as it is exactly the uniqueness of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow in the case
|I|=|O|
(cf. Corollary
4.6
) that makes the insertion of
Z
-like vertices and the corresponding updates to the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow possible while performing fewer
3
3

3

In fact: strictly fewer, unless the lower bound from Theorem
G.2
is also an upper bound.
operations than would be required by the brute-force approach of inserting the vertex and running the full flow-finding procedure
[
3
, page 109]
.

Theorem 6.6

(
Z
-like insertion)
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, where
|I|=|O|
.
Further suppose that
C,NC
are known, where
C
is the reduced correction matrix guaranteed to exist by Theorem
4.5
and to be unique by Corollary
4.6
; and
N
is the order-demand matrix of
\Gamma
.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
be the open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph extended by
v
via a known vector
S\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{V}
.
Then, in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time complexity, it is possible to determine the set of all
\lambda^{\prime}(v)
such that
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)\neq 0
and
(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

6.4
Generation of labelled open graphs with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

Benchmarking flow-based algorithms on qubits, such as finding and circuit extraction, already requires generating large labelled open graphs with flow, and such labelled open graphs have also found application in the generation of ansätze for optimisation and machine learning
[
18
]
.
For these applications, the flows should be
non-trivial
, i.e. the correction mechanism should not be obvious from the structure of the graphs alone.
Since most labelled open graphs do not have flow
4
4

4

Informally, most flow-demand matrices will not have maximal row-rank and thus have no right inverse. In particular, a certain submatrix of the adjacency matrix needs to be invertible, but most graphs do not have this property
[
38
]
.
, it can be difficult to generate instances that have flow in a non-trivial way.
For example, one way to generate large instances with flow on qubits is to translate quantum circuits into measurement patterns.
However, this method results in diagrams with causal flow, where the correction mechanism is particularly simple: each row of the correction-matrix contains only a single non-zero value.
Another approach for generating labelled open graph s with flow is to compose smaller instances with flow.
However, this leads to a layered structure with limited connectivity.
Based on a recent result of one of the authors for qubits
[
5
]
, we now give an algorithmic method for generating instances with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow that can overcome these shortcomings: the algorithm has non-zero probability of returning
any
labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, not only labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graphs of the restricted types outlined above.

Theorem 6.7
.

There exists an algorithm (presented in Appendix
H.5
) that takes a pair of integers
n
and
k
as input and it returns a random labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
|V|=n
and
|I|=|O|=k
that has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Furthermore, for any labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
\Gamma
with
|V|=n
and
|I|=|O|=k
with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, the algorithm has non-zero probability of returning
\Gamma
.

The proof is in Appendix
H.5
.
The method we use may not be very efficient, as it is based on attempts of
Z
-like insertions, which can show that insertion of a vertex with a fixed neighbourhood is simply impossible in an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving manner.
However, the method is guaranteed to return cases with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Thus, a one-time generation could be run for an extended period to produce instances that can then be used for benchmarking flow algorithm implementations.
This method is more elegant than the trivial approach of generating large labelled open graphs and running the flow-finding algorithm.
We also expect it to be more practical for generating cases with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, though we leave the last fact without a formal proof.

7
Conclusions and future directions

Building on the work of
[
8
]
about determinism in qudit MBQC, we developed a definition of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
We showed that the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is equivalent to the existence of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow; the uniqueness of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow (at least when the numbers of inputs and outputs are equal) thus means focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow can be considered canonical.
We refined the algebraic formulation to allow a restatement of the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition using matrix multiplication and a check whether a certain directed graph is acyclic, thus avoiding the previous complicated verification of conditions related to the order of measurements.
Utilising this new algebraic formulation, we obtained an
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})

\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding algorithm, the same complexity as for qubits.
Furthermore, we laid the groundwork for applications of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow in optimisation by deriving several new
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving rules.

The most natural direction for future work is to apply our results in optimisation.
All properties of flow and flow-preserving transformations used in
[
17
]
have now been generalised to higher dimensions suggesting that circuit optimisation procedure over qubits has a great potential to lift to qudits.
Furthermore, our results also generalise flow results essential in refinements of this optimisation method, for instance in
[
26
]
, albeit some results that do not concern flow remain to be generalised.

Another direction for the future work would be to develop a ‘causal
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow’ analogous to
[
14
]
.
While causal flow is not necessary for determinism in the qubit case, it corresponds more closely to the circuit model than gflow, which can be advantageous for certain applications
[
24
]
.
Moreover causal flow can be found in sub-cubic time
[
15
,
16
]
.
Additionally, an extended version of causal flow plays a core role in the completeness result for flow-preserving rewriting on qubits
[
2
]
, which could potentially be extended to qudits in the future.

In the qubit case, separate treatment of Pauli measurement leads to a different flow
[
11
]
with its own applications
[
39
]
and algebraic presentation
[
32
,
31
]
; all
while offering a broader picture of determinism than gflow
[
11
,
30
]
.
It would be interesting to investigate the existence of a Pauli flow for qudits.

Lastly, our techniques exploit the field structure of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
for an improved algebraic presentation.
In the present work on qudits and its predecessor
[
8
]
, these fields are necessarily finite.
There also exists a definition of MBQC and an associated flow on quantum systems described by continuous variables
[
9
]
, which effectively works over the infinite field
\mathbb{R}
.
We expect our algebraic approach could be adjusted and applied to this continuous variable flow, however the infinite field might lead to difficulties on the algorithmic side of this work.
Care would be needed to choose a suitable representation of real numbers that permits efficient computation of relevant field operations.

Acknowledgements

We thank Robert Booth for helpful discussions about
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow. PM is supported by Niedersächsisches Ministerium für Wissenschaft und Kultur. MB is supported by the Plan France 2030 through the PEPR integrated project EPiQ ANR-22-PETQ-0007 and the HQI platform ANR-22-PNCQ-0002.

Appendix A
Comparison to the qubit case

Qubit MBQC and its flow properties are more widely studied and thus many readers might be familiar with qubit flow, but not qudit flow.
For such readers, in this appendix, we explain how various definitions and results correspond to the qubit-case literature, i.e. to the case of
d=2
.
In particular, when
d=2
, all matrices become binary matrices.
From now on, we only focus on differences regarding notation, terminology, or provision of literature reference when
d=2
.
We skip over statements where
d=2
implies all changes on its own.

Background

•

The
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Definition
2.1
becomes a simple graph. In particular, it is no longer weighted.

•

The codomain of the measurement labelling associated with a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph (Definition
2.3
) codomain would become
\{(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)\}
.
However, all three measurement spaces are known in the literature under different names:

–

The
(0,1)
space becomes the
XY
-plane, i.e. the plane spanned by the
X
and
Y
eigenstates; this is the plane of the Bloch sphere perpendicular to the
Z
axis,

–

the
(1,0)
space becomes the
YZ
-plane defined analogously, and

–

the
(1,1)
space becomes the
XZ
-plane, again defined analogously.

The condition that all measured inputs must be measured in the
(0,*)
space translates to a similar condition involving the
XY
-plane.

•

The single qubit Paulis become
X
,
Y
, and
Z
only (no nontrivial powers needed), where
Y\propto XZ
.

•

The
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow from Definition
2.7
becomes the gflow of
[
11
]
; a detailed translation is explained in
[
8
]
.
The translation requires mapping the correction matrix
C
to a correction function
c
by associating columns of
C
with set indicator functions, i.e.
c(v)=\{u\mid C_{u,v}=1\}
.

Focusing
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

•

The notion of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow collapses to that of focused gflow from
[
1
]
.

•

The existence of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow generalises the existence of focused gflow from
[
29
, Section 3.1]
and
[
1
]
.

Algebraic interpretation of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

•

The reduced adjacency matrix from Definition
4.1
has been considered over qubits in
[
29
, Definition 6]
.

•

The definitions of the flow-demand matrix (Definition
4.3
) and the order-demand matrix (Definition
4.4
) become the similarly-named matrices over qubits from
[
31
, Definition 3.4 and Definition 3.5]
.

•

The new algebraic formulation from Theorem
4.5
becomes the main result from our previous work
[
31
, Theorem 3.1]
.
In particular,
NC
is no longer a weighted graph.

•

The uniqueness of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow when
|I|=|O|
(Corollary
4.6
), becomes the uniqueness of gflow under the same restriction from
[
1
]
.

•

The focused vectors of Definition
4.7
become the focused sets from
[
39
, Definition 4.3]
.
Originally, focused sets were defined in the setting of Pauli flow, yet they apply just as well to gflow.

•

Theorem
4.8
becomes
[
31
, Theorem 3.23]
.

Flow-finding

The correspondence between qudit
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding and qubit flow-finding has been carefully explained in Section
5
and its corresponding appendices.

\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving rewriting

•

Regarding the qubit equivalents of the operations in Section
6.1
, local scaling is trivial and local complementations are not parametrised.
Unlike for qudits, there are actually two variants of local complementation on a qubit, which differ by a non-trivial graph stabiliser; conversely, one variant corresponds to three successive applications of the other about the same qubit.
The definition of the qubit pivot uses both variants.
Flow preservation of local complementation and pivoting for gflow was proved in
[
1
, Lemma 3.1 and Corollary 3.3]
.

•

Flow reversal in Theorem
6.3
translates to
[
31
, Theorem 3.19]
.

•

The notion of ‘
Z
-like measurements’ from Section
6.3
captures
YZ
- and
XZ
-measured vertices only, since we are not treating Pauli vertices separately and hence there are no
Z
measurements.

•

Z
-like removal from Theorem
6.5
corresponds to
[
1
, Lemma 3.4]
.

•

Z
-like insertion from Theorem
6.6
corresponds to results from
[
3
, Section 4 and Section 5]
.

•

Generation of instances with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow from Theorem
6.7
translates to a result about generating MBQC patterns with different types of flow from the upcoming paper
[
5
]
.

Appendix B
Proving focusing

To prove Theorem
3.2
, we first prove that a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph has focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow if and only if it has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, by giving a procedure that allows any
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow to be modified in a stepwise fashion until it is focused, with each step being a valid
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
This procedure generalises
[
1
, Section 3.3]
to the qudit case.
First, we specify a definition of focusing that applies to a single correction set and a single vertex.

Definition B.1
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
, and let
u,v\in V
be two distinct vertices, i.e.
u\neq v
.
We say the correction
C_{*,u}
is focused with respect to
v
if the following two statements hold:

(FXI)

v\in O\vee(C_{v,v}=0\implies(GC)_{v,u}=0)
, and

(FZI)

C_{v,v}\neq 0\implies C_{v,u}=0
.

Observation B.2
.

An
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is focused according to Definition
3.1
if and only if
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
hold for all distinct pairs
u,v\in V
.
Indeed, if Definition
B.1
holds for all distinct pairs of vertices
u,v
, then Definition
3.1
is immediate.

For the converse, note that if
v\in O
, then
C_{v,v}=0
by
(ZF)
, so both
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
are satisfied for any
C_{*,u}
.
Similarly, if
(C_{v,u},(GC)_{v,u})=(0,0)
, then
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
are satisfied for
C_{*,u}
and
v
.
In particular, if
u\in O
, then
C_{w,u}=0
for all
w\in V
by
(ZF)
; this then also implies
(GC)_{v,u}=\sum_{w\in V}G_{v,w}C_{w,u}=0
.

Recall that if
v\in\bar{O}
, then
\lambda_{X}(v)=C_{v,v}
by
(CF)
.
Thus, if an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
is focused according to Definition
3.1
, then
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
hold for all distinct pairs
u,v\in\bar{O}
.
Moreover, the two conditions hold whenever at least one of
u,v
is in
O
by the argument above.
Thus, the two conditions hold for all pairs of distinct vertices.

We now show how to focus a flow in a step-wise fashion.
The first argument is to show the construction that focuses a new correction set with respect to some chosen vertex, without breaking focusing conditions that already hold for other vertices.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
, and let
u,v\in V
be two distinct vertices, i.e.
u\neq v
.
Then we can construct an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec)
on
(G,I,O,\lambda)
such that
C^{\prime}_{*,u}
is focused with respect to
v
.
Moreover, the construction ensures that if there exists a vertex
v^{\prime}\neq v
such that
C_{*,v}
is focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
, then, for any
w\in V
,
C^{\prime}_{*,w}
is focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
if and only if
C_{*,w}
is focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Observation
B.2
,
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
are satisfied if at least one of
u,v
is an output, or if
(C_{v,u},(GC)_{v,u})=(0,0)
; in those cases we are done by taking
C^{\prime}=C
and the second part of the lemma is trivial.
Hence it only remains to consider the case where
u,v\in\bar{O}
and
(C_{v,u},(GC)_{v,u})\neq(0,0)
; by
(OF)
this implies
u\prec v
.
We distinguish cases according to whether or not
C_{v,v}
is 0.

•

Suppose
C_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, then
(FZI)
is trivially satisfied.
If
(GC)_{v,u}=0
,
(FXI)
is also satisfied and we are done by taking
C^{\prime}=C
.

Otherwise, note that by
(CF)
and Definition
2.3
we have
\lambda_{Z}(v)=(GC)_{v,v}\neq 0
.
Define a new correction matrix
C^{\prime}
as

C^{\prime}_{w,w^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}C_{w,w^{\prime}}&\text{if }w^{\prime}\neq u\\
C_{w,u}+\frac{d-(GC)_{v,u}}{\lambda_{Z}(v)}C_{w,v}&\text{if }w^{\prime}=u.\end{cases}

Then by linearity:

(GC^{\prime})_{w,w^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}(GC)_{w,w^{\prime}}&\text{if }w^{\prime}\neq u\\
(GC)_{w,u}+\frac{d-(GC)_{v,u}}{\lambda_{Z}(v)}(GC)_{w,v}&\text{if }w^{\prime}=u.\end{cases}

In particular,
(GC^{\prime})_{v,u}=(GC)_{v,u}+\frac{d-(GC)_{v,u}}{\lambda_{Z}(v)}\lambda_{Z}(v)=0
over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
, meaning
(FXI)
is satisfied.
Furthermore,
(C^{\prime},\prec)
satisfies Definition
2.7
:

–

The only column of
C
that has changed is
C_{*,u}
.
We have
C_{u,v}=0=(GC)_{u,v}
by
(OF)
since
(GC)_{v,u}\neq 0
; thus
(C^{\prime}_{u,u},(GC^{\prime})_{u,u})=(C_{u,u},(GC)_{u,u})
and
(CF)
is satisfied.

–

If
w^{\prime}\in O
, then
w^{\prime}\neq u
and thus
C^{\prime}_{w,w^{\prime}}=C_{w,w^{\prime}}
.
Now
C_{w,w^{\prime}}=0
by
(ZF)
applied to
C
, so the desired property holds for
C^{\prime}
as well.
Similarly, if
w\in I
, then
C_{w,u}=C_{w,v}=0
by
(ZF)
applied to
C
and thus
C^{\prime}_{w,u}=0
.
Hence
C^{\prime}
satisfies
(ZF)
.

–

Suppose
C^{\prime}_{w,u}\neq 0
for some
w
, then
C_{w,u}\neq 0
or
C_{w,v}\neq 0
.
In the former case,
u\prec w
and in the latter case
u\prec v\prec w
.
The argument is analogous for
GC^{\prime}
, thus
(OF)
is satisfied.

Hence
(C^{\prime},\prec)
is a valid
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

•

Now suppose instead
C_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, then
(FXI)
is trivially satisfied.
If
C_{v,u}=0
, then
(FZI)
is also satisfied and we are done.
Otherwise, define a new correction matrix
C^{\prime}
as

C^{\prime}_{w,w^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}C_{w,w^{\prime}}&\text{if }w^{\prime}\neq u\\
C_{w,u}+\frac{d-C_{v,u}}{\lambda_{X}(v)}C_{w,v}&\text{if }w^{\prime}=u.\end{cases}

Then
C_{v,u}=C_{v,u}+\frac{d-C_{v,u}}{\lambda_{X}(v)}\lambda_{X}(v)=0
over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
, meaning
(FZI)
is satisfied.
The argument that
(C^{\prime},\prec)
is a valid
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is analogous to the one in the previous case.

We have shown how to satisfy
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
for the pair
u,v
.
Now suppose there exists
v^{\prime}\neq v
such that
C_{*,v}
is focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
v^{\prime}\in\bar{O}
, otherwise
(FXI)
and
(FZI)
are trivial by Observation
B.2
.
From the argument that
(C^{\prime},\prec)
is a valid
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow at the end of the first case, we know that
C^{\prime}_{v^{\prime},v^{\prime}}=C_{v^{\prime},v^{\prime}}
.
Now, for any
w\in V
, we have
C^{\prime}_{v^{\prime},w}=C_{v^{\prime},w}+\ell C_{v^{\prime},v}
, where

\ell=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }w\neq u\\
\frac{d-(GC)_{v,u}}{\lambda_{Z}(v)}&\text{if }w=u\text{ and }\lambda_{X}(v)=C_{v,v}=0\\
\frac{d-C_{v,u}}{\lambda_{X}(v)}&\text{if }w=u\text{ and }\lambda_{X}(v)=C_{v,v}\neq 0.\end{cases}

As
u,v
are fixed, the value of
\ell
depends only on
w
.
Furthermore, by linearity,
(GC^{\prime})_{v^{\prime},w}=(GC^{\prime})_{v^{\prime},w}+\ell(GC^{\prime})_{v^{\prime},v}
.
Thus:

•

If
\lambda_{X}(v^{\prime})=C_{v^{\prime},v^{\prime}}=0
, then
(FZI)
is trivial.
Moreover,
C_{*,v}
being focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
implies
C_{v^{\prime},v}=0
.
Thus, for all
w\in V
, we have
C^{\prime}_{v^{\prime},w}=C_{v^{\prime},w}+\ell C_{v^{\prime},v}=C_{v^{\prime},w}
, so
C^{\prime}_{*,w}
is focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
if and only if
C_{*,w}
is.

•

If
C_{v^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\neq 0
, then
(FXI)
is trivial.
Moreover,
C_{*,v}
being focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
implies
(GC)_{v^{\prime},v}=0
.
Thus, for all
w\in V
, we have
(GC^{\prime})_{v^{\prime},w}=(GC)_{v^{\prime},w}+\ell(GC)_{v^{\prime},v}=(GC)_{v^{\prime},w}
, so again
C^{\prime}_{*,w}
is focused with respect to
v^{\prime}
if and only if
C_{*,w}
is.

This completes the proof.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
3.2
:

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

By Observation
B.2
, instead of working with Definition
3.1
, we may consider Definition
B.1
for all pairs of distinct
u,v\in V
.
A focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow can thus be focused using the following procedure.

Let
<
be any totalisation of
\prec
; for simplicity we will from now on identify
V
with the set
[n]:=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, where
n=\left|V\right|
.
We will prove the following property by structural induction on
k
ranging from 1 to
n
:

There exists an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec)
such that for all
v\in[n]\setminus[n-k]
and all
u\neq v
,
C^{\prime}_{*,u}
is focused with respect to
v
.

The base case is
k=1
, and the property holds for
C^{\prime}:=C
since
C_{u,n}=0=(GC)_{u,n}
for any
u<n
by
(OF)
.
Now suppose the property holds for some
k
such that
1\leq k<n
and denote the corresponding
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow by
(C,\prec)
.
Consider what happens for
k+1
.
Let
a:=n-(k+1)
and define the following sequence of correction matrices:
C^{(0)}:=C
and for
j\in[a-1]
,

C^{(j)}_{u,v}:=\begin{cases}C^{(j-1)}_{u,v}&\text{if }v\neq j\\
C^{(j-1)}_{u,j}+\frac{d-(GC^{(j-1)})_{a,j}}{(GC^{(j-1)})_{a,a}}C^{(j-1)}_{u,a}&\text{if }v=j\text{ and }C^{(j-1)}_{a,a}=0\\
C^{(j-1)}_{u,j}+\frac{d-C^{(j-1)}_{a,j}}{C^{(j-1)}_{a,a}}C^{(j-1)}_{u,a}&\text{if }v=j\text{ and }C^{(j-1)}_{a,a}\neq 0.\end{cases}

Note that if
C^{(j-1)}_{*,j}
is already focused with respect to
a
, then
C^{(j)}=C^{(j-1)}
.
Then by Lemma
B.3
,
C^{(j)}_{*,j}
is focused with respect to
a
.
Moreover, by the same lemma,
C^{(j)}_{*,b}
is focused with respect to
a
for all
b\in[j]
, and together with the induction hypothesis, for any
j\in[a-1]
, any
v>a
, and any
u\neq v
, we have that
C^{(j)}_{*,u}
is focused with respect to
v
.
Finally,
C^{(a-1)}_{*,u}
is focused with respect to
a
for all
u>a
since
(OF)
implies that
C^{(a-1)}_{a,u}=(GC^{(a-1)})_{a,u}=0
.
Thus the property holds for
k+1
with correction matrix
C^{\prime}=C^{(a-1)}
.

This means the property holds for
k=n
.
The final
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec)
has the property that for all
v\in V
and all
u\in V\setminus\{v\}
, the correction
C^{\prime}_{*,u}
is focused with respect to
v
.
By Observation
B.2
, this means
(C^{\prime},\prec)
satisfies Definition
3.1
, i.e. it is focused.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs regarding algebraic formulation

The main result shown in this appendix is Theorem
4.5
, but we also prove some related results here.
In Subsection
C.1
, we lay ground for the main proof.
Next, in Subsection
C.2
, we provide detailed proof of Theorem
4.5
.
Lastly, in Subsection
C.3
, we prove some properties of the focused vectors that are associated with the new algebraic formulation.

C.1
Preliminary simplifications

Before moving to the main proof, we perform some initial simplification by showing that the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition can be restricted to operate only on the reduced adjacency matrix rather than the full matrix.
Moreover, we define a special relation induced by the correction matrix, which is sufficient to determine whether that correction matrix is compatible with any
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, thus eliminating the need to consider arbitrary partial orders.

Note that Condition
(C)
from Definition
4.2
can also be simplified to
\forall u\in\bar{O}.\lambda(u)=(C_{u,u},(AC)_{u,u})
under the convention that non-existent entries of a matrix are treated as
0
s.
Such a non-existent entry appears e.g. for
C_{u,u}
with
u\in I
.
We will now show that the simplified
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition is equivalent to the original one.

Lemma C.1
.

There exists
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow according to Definition
2.7
if and only if there exists
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow according to Definition
4.2
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow):
suppose there exists an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
on the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
according to Definition
2.7
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the reduced correction matrix obtained from
C
and let
\prec^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\prec
to an order on
\bar{O}
only.
This restriction is immediately a strict partial order.
Now, from
(ZF)
we know
C_{i,v}=0
for all
i\in I
and
C_{v,o}=0
for all
o\in O
(with
v\in V
arbitrary), which implies
C^{\prime}
contains all non-zero entries of
C
.
As all the non-zero entries are preserved, we furthermore have
(AC)_{u,v}=(GC)_{u,v}
for all
u\in\bar{O}
and
v\in\bar{I}
.
Therefore, condition
(OF)
implies
(O)
, and
(CF)
implies
(C)
.
As a consequence,
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec^{\prime})
according to Definition
4.2
.

(\Leftarrow):
suppose there exists an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec^{\prime})
on the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
according to Definition
4.2
.
Let
C
be the extension of
C^{\prime}
to a matrix of shape
V\times V
by filling all missing entries with
0
: this immediately makes
C
satisfy
(ZF)
.
Let
\prec
be the extension of
\prec^{\prime}
to an order on
V
by adding relations
u\prec o
for all
u\in\bar{O}
and
o\in O
.
This process makes
\prec
a strict partial order.
Similar to the proof above,
(AC)_{u,v}=(GC)_{u,v}
for all
u\in\bar{O}
and
v\in\bar{I}
as once again
C^{\prime}
contains all non-zero entries of
C
.
Therefore condition
(C)
implies
(CF)
.
The matrices
C^{\prime}
and
AC^{\prime}
are lower-triangular according to any totalisation of
\prec^{\prime}
, hence
C
and
GC
are lower triangular according to any totalisation of
\prec
, because
{\prec^{\prime}}\subset{\prec}
and rows and columns of
C
not featured in
C^{\prime}
are identically
0
.
In other words, condition
(O)
implies
(OF)
.
Thus
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
according to Definition
2.7
.
∎

The definition of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow (cf. Definition
3.1
) is compatible with both
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definitions.
Thus, also, the existence of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow with respect to one
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition is equivalent to the existence of focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow with respect to the other
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition:

Corollary C.2
.

There exists focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow according to Definition
2.7
if and only if there exists focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow according to Definition
4.2
.

The usual flow definitions simultaneously describe the correction function and the strict partial order.
Yet the conditions
(FX)
and
(FZ)
do not depend on the strict partial order, but only on the correction matrix.
Thus, it makes sense to introduce the notion of a ‘focused correction matrix’, dropping the partial order from the definition:

Definition C.3

(Focused correction matrix)
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and
C
a reduced correction matrix. We say that
C
is
focused
if it satisfies
(FX)
and
(FZ)
.

Definition C.4

(Extensive correction matrix)
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a reduced correction matrix on
\Gamma
.
We call
C

extensive
when there exists a strict partial order
\prec
such that
(C,\prec)
is an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow on
\Gamma
.
We call
C

focused extensive
when there exists
\prec
such that
(C,\prec)
is a focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow on
\Gamma
.

When determining whether a correction matrix is extensive, one must determine whether there exists a strict partial order that satisfies condition
(O)
from Definition
4.2
.
It turns out that, for any correction matrix, there exists a unique relation that determines extensivity.

Definition C.5

(Induced relation)
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix on
\Gamma
.
The
induced relation

\triangleleft_{C}
is the coarsest relation on
\bar{O}
such that for any pair of distinct
u,v\in\bar{O}
we have
u\triangleleft_{C}v
if and only if at least one of the following hold:

(O1)

(AC)_{v,u}\neq 0
, or

(O2)

C_{v,u}\neq 0
.

When
\triangleleft_{C}
is an acyclic relation, we denote the transitive closure of
\triangleleft_{C}
as
\prec_{C}
.

The entries of the product
AC
do not depend on any ordering of the vertices, and thus the above definition is sound.
The relation
\triangleleft_{C}
captures all information regarding which off-diagonal entries of
AC
are non-zero, motivating the following lemma:

Lemma C.6

(Induced order containment)
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
(C,\prec)
be an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Then
{\prec_{C}}\subseteq{\prec}
.

Proof.

Let
\prec^{\prime}
be any totalisation of
\prec
.
By Definition
4.2
, both
AC
and
C
must be lower triangular with respect to the ordering
\prec^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
u\prec_{C}v
.
By Definition
C.5
,
(AC)_{v,u}\neq 0
or
C_{v,u}\neq 0
.
Now if
v\prec^{\prime}u
, then either
AC
or
C
would not be lower triangular, a contradiction.
Since
\prec^{\prime}
is a total order and
\neg(v\prec^{\prime}u)
, we get that necessarily
u\prec^{\prime}v
.
Repeating for all elements of
\prec_{C}
, we find that
{\prec_{C}}\subseteq{\prec^{\prime}}
.
This inclusion must hold for all totalisations
\prec^{\prime}
of
\prec
and thus
\prec_{C}
forms a subset of the intersection of all such totalisations.
This intersection is precisely
\prec
, ending the proof.
∎

We now have all the parts needed to prove that an extensive correction matrix determines a valid
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

Theorem C.7

(Extending a correction matrix to an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow)
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix on
\Gamma
. Then
C
is (focused) extensive if and only if
(C,\prec_{C})
is a (focused)
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
: Suppose that
C
is extensive.
Then there exists a strict partial order
\prec
on
\bar{O}
such that
(C,\prec)
is an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
By Lemma
C.6
,
{\prec_{C}}\subseteq{\prec}
.
Thus, since
\prec
is a strict partial order,
\prec_{C}
is also a strict partial order.
Since
C
is extensive, it must satisfy condition
(C)
(which does not depend on the partial order).
Similarly, if
C
is focused extensive, it must necessarily satisfy conditions
(FX)
and
(FZ)
, which are also independent of the partial order.
It remains to show that
(C,\prec_{C})
satisfies condition
(O)
.
Suppose for a contradiction that
(O)
fails for
\prec_{C}
.
Then, for some totalisation
\prec_{C}^{\prime}
of
\prec_{C}
, we must have
v\prec_{C}^{\prime}u
and either
C_{v,u}\neq 0
or
(AC)_{v,u}\neq 0
.
However, either of
C_{v,u}
or
(AC)_{v,u}
being non-zero implies
u\prec_{C}v
and thus
u\prec_{C}^{\prime}v
.
This contradicts the assumption
v\prec_{C}^{\prime}u
.
Thus
(C,\prec_{C})
satisfies
(O)
and hence forms an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, which moreover is focused if and only if
C
is focused extensive.

(\Leftarrow)
: Immediate from the definitions.
∎

Theorem
C.7
states that in order to determine whether
C
is (focused) extensive, it is sufficient to check whether
(C,\prec_{C})
forms a (focused)
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
In fact, based on the proof, a slightly stronger property holds.
If
C
satisfies condition
(C)
(and conditions
(FX)
and
(FZ)
) and
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order, then
(C,\prec_{C})
is a (focused)
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, effectively skipping verification of condition
(O)
apart from ensuring that
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order.
This allows us to avoid considering arbitrary totalisations of
\prec_{C}
when verifying the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow conditions.
Furthermore, since
\prec_{C}
is a transitive closure of
\triangleleft_{C}
,
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order if and only if
\triangleleft_{C}
forms a directed acyclic graph.
Together, this amounts to the following simpler algebraic formulation of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow:

Theorem C.8
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be any correction matrix on
\Gamma
.
Then
C
is extensive if and only if
C
satisfies condition
(C)
and
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order.
Assuming the above holds,
C
is focused extensive if and only if it satisfies conditions
(FX)
and
(FZ)
.

C.2
Main proof

We now move on to proving the main result, Theorem
4.5
.
The first step is to split condition
(C)
into several smaller parts that will be encoded in different parts of the algebraic
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow definition:

Observation C.9
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix of shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
.
Define the following conditions for all
v\in\bar{O}
:

(C1)

\lambda_{X}(v)=0\rightarrow(AC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{Z}(v)

(C2)

\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0\rightarrow C_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)

(C3)

\lambda_{X}(v)=0\rightarrow C_{v,v}=0

(C4)

\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0\rightarrow(AC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{Z}(v)

Then,
(C)
is equivalent to the four above conditions, as
\lambda_{X}(v)=C_{v,v}
is captured by
(C2)
and
(C3)
, while
\lambda_{Z}(v)=(AC)_{v,v}
is captured by
(C1)
and
(C4)
.

The goal is to show that
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
corresponds to
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(FX)
, and
(FZ)
; while
NC
being a DAG corresponds to
(C3)
,
(C4)
, and the condition that
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order.
We split the proof of this into two lemmata:

Lemma C.10
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix of shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
.
Then
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
if and only if
C
satisfies
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(FX)
, and
(FZ)
.

Proof.

First, consider some relevant properties of the entries of
M
in relation to the entries of
A
and
C
.
Let
v\in\bar{O}
and
u\in\bar{I}
, then by Definition
4.3
:

M_{v,u}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}A_{v,u}&\text{if}\ \lambda_{X}(v)=0\\
\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}\delta_{v,u}&\text{if}\ \lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0.\end{cases}

Let
w\in\bar{O}
and consider two cases depending on
\lambda_{X}(v)
:

1.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
and thus
M_{v,u}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}A_{v,u}
.
Multiplying by
C_{u,w}
, we get:

M_{v,u}C_{u,w}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}A_{v,u}C_{u,w}

and hence by linearity:

(MC)_{v,w}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}(AC)_{v,w}

(1)

2.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
and thus
M_{v,u}=\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}\delta_{v,u}
.
When
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, then necessarily
v\notin I
and thus
M
has a
v
-column.
Multiplying
M_{v,u}
be
{C_{u,w}}
, we get:

M_{v,u}C_{u,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}\delta_{v,u}C_{u,w}

which equals
0
unless
v=u
when it equals
\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}C_{v,w}
.
Using this fact, we find that:

(MC)_{v,w}=\sum_{u\in\bar{I}}M_{v,u}C_{u,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}C_{v,w}

(2)

Now we can split the proof into two directions.

(\Rightarrow):
Suppose
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
; this implies
(MC)_{v,v}=1
and
(MC)_{v,w}=0
for all
w\in\bar{O}\setminus\{v\}
.
The following hold:

1.

For all
v
such that
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, by Equation (
1
), we have
1=(MC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}(AC)_{v,v}
.
Thus
(AC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{Z}(v)
, i.e.
(C1)
holds.

2.

Furthermore, for all
v
such that
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
and for all
w
such that
v\neq w
, by Equation (
1
) we have
0=(MC)_{v,w}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}(AC)_{v,w}
.
Since
\lambda_{Z}(v)\neq 0
(as
\lambda(v)\neq(0,0)
), this implies
(AC)_{v,w}=0
and thus
(FX)
holds.

3.

For all
v
such that
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, by Equation (
2
), we have
1=(MC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}C_{v,v}
.
Thus
C_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)
, i.e.
(C2)
holds.

4.

Finally, for all
v
such that
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
and for all
w
such that
v\neq w
, by Equation
2
we have
0=(MC)_{v,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}C_{v,w}
.
This implies
C_{v,w}=0
and thus
(FZ)
holds.

Hence the desired properties are all satisfied.

(\Leftarrow):
Suppose the four properties hold.
We must show that
(MC)_{v,v}=1
and
(MC)_{v,w}=0
for all
v\neq w\in\bar{O}
.
Observe that all four points in the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof are reversible.
Therefore
(MC)_{v,v}=1
follows from reversing the two first points of the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof and
(MC)_{v,w}=0
follows from reversing the latter two points of the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof.
Then
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
as desired.
∎

The proof for the product
NC
being a DAG follows the same structure.
We split it up into three parts for simplicity.
Before proving those lemmata, first note that for any
v,w\in\bar{O}
, by Definition
4.4
:

N_{v,u}=\lambda_{X}(v)A_{v,u}-\lambda_{Z}(v)\delta_{v,u}

for any
u\in\bar{I}
.
Since
\delta_{v,u}=0
unless
u=v
, we get the following:

\displaystyle(NC)_{v,w}=\sum_{u\in\bar{I}}N_{v,u}C_{u,w}=\left(\sum_{u\in\bar{I}}\lambda_{X}(v)A_{v,u}C_{u,w}\right)-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,w}-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,w}

(3)

where we use the convention
C_{v,w}=0
for
v\in I
to make sense of the final expression despite the rows of
NC
being labelled by
\bar{O}
and the rows of
C
being labelled by
\bar{I}
.

Lemma C.11
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix of shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
satisfying
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(FX)
, and
(FZ)
.
Then the main diagonal of
NC
is identically zero if and only if
(C3)
and
(C4)
hold.

Proof.

Fix
v=w\in\bar{O}
in (
3
) and consider two cases depending on whether
\lambda_{X}(v)
is zero:

1.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
.
Then
(NC)_{v,v}=-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,v}
which equals
0
if and only if
C_{v,v}=0
.
Considering all
v
with
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, we obtain equivalence of
(NC)_{v,v}=0
to
(C3)
.

2.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
.
By the assumption that
C
satisfies
(C2)
, we have
C_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)
.
Then:

(NC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,v}-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,v}-\lambda_{Z}(v)\lambda_{X}(v)=\lambda_{X}(v)((AC)_{v,v}-\lambda_{Z}(v))

which equals
0
if and only if
(AC)_{v,v}=\lambda_{Z}(v)
.
Considering all
v
with
\lambda_{X}\neq 0
, we obtain equivalence of
(NC)_{v,v}=0
to
(C4)
.

Thus, the above two cases imply that the main diagonal of
NC
is identically zero if and only if
(C3)
and
(C4)
hold.
∎

Lemma C.12
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix of shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
satisfying
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(FX)
, and
(FZ)
.
Then the off-diagonal part of
NC
corresponds to
\triangleleft_{C}
in the sense that, for any pair of distinct
v,w\in\bar{O}
, we have
w\triangleleft_{C}v
if and only if
(NC)_{v,w}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let
v,w\in\bar{O}
be distinct.
We show that
w\triangleleft_{C}v
if and only if
(NC)_{v,w}\neq 0
.

(\Rightarrow)
: Suppose that
w\triangleleft_{C}v
.
As before, we consider two cases depending on whether
\lambda_{X}(v)
is zero:

1.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
.
By the assumption that
C
satisfies
(FX)
, we have
(AC)_{v,w}=0
.
Thus the condition of
(O1)
does not hold.
Ergo the condition of
(O2)
necessarily applies, leading to
C_{v,w}\neq 0
.
Therefore (
3
) becomes:

(NC)_{v,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,w}-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,w}=-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,w}\neq 0

2.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
.
By the assumption that
C
satisfies
(FZ)
, we have
C_{v,w}=0
.
Thus the condition of
(O2)
does not apply.
Ergo the condition of
(O1)
necessarily applies, leading to
(AC)_{v,w}\neq 0
.
Therefore (
3
) becomes:

(NC)_{v,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,w}-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,w}=\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,w}\neq 0
