In both cases,
(NC)_{v,w}\neq 0
as desired.

(\Leftarrow)
: Suppose that
(NC)_{v,w}\neq 0
, which means:

\lambda_{X}(v)(AC)_{v,w}-\lambda_{Z}(v)C_{v,w}\neq 0

If both
(AC)_{v,w}=0
and
C_{v,w}=0
, the above cannot hold.
Thus at least one of conditions
(O1)
or
(O2)
applies, and we get
w\triangleleft_{C}v
.

Therefore the off-diagonal part of
NC
corresponds to
\triangleleft_{C}
.
∎

Lemma C.13
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
C
be a correction matrix of shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
satisfying
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(FX)
, and
(FZ)
.
Then
NC
forms a (weighted) directed acyclic graph if and only if
C
satisfies
(C3)
,
(C4)
, and
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order.

Proof.

Let
v,w\in\bar{O}
.
We combine the previous two lemmata to get the full proof.

(\Rightarrow)
: Under the assumption that
NC
forms a (weighted) DAG, by Lemma
C.12
, we get that
\triangleleft_{C}
is a DAG and thus
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order.
Furthermore,
NC
being a DAG implies
(NC)_{v,v}=0
for all
v\in\bar{O}
and thus, by Lemma
C.11
, conditions
(C3)
and
(C4)
both hold.

(\Leftarrow)
:
(C3)
and
(C4)
imply that
(NC)_{v,v}=0
by Lemma
C.11
and
\prec_{C}
being a strict partial order implies that
\triangleleft_{C}
is a DAG.
The latter implies that the off-diagonal part of
NC
is a DAG by Lemma
C.12
; by combining the two parts the entire matrix
NC
forms a DAG.
∎

We can now prove the main result.

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

(\Rightarrow)
: suppose that
\Gamma
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Then by Theorem
3.2
it has a focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C,\prec)
and by Theorem
C.7

(C,\prec_{C})
is also a focused flow.
Hence, by Theorem
C.8
,
C
satisfies
(C)
,
(FX)
,
(FZ)
, and
\prec_{C}
is a strict partial order.
By Observation
C.9
,
C
satisfies
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(C3)
, and
(C4)
.
Therefore, by Lemma
C.10
we get
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
, and by Lemma
C.13
we get that
NC
forms a (weighted) DAG.

(\Leftarrow)
: all steps in the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof follow from if and only if statements and thus the above argument can be reversed.
∎

C.3
Proofs regarding focused vectors

Before proving Theorem
4.8
, we first show that given
\vec{\mathscr{a}}\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{I}}
, one can find a maximal set over which
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is focused (cf. Definition
4.7
) by considering the product
M\vec{\mathscr{a}}
, where
M
is the flow-demand matrix.
Note that the definition of focused vectors involves the reduced adjacency matrix
A
, whereas we now use the flow-demand matrix; this change is justified in the following lemma.

Lemma C.14
.

Let
(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and let
\vec{\mathscr{a}}\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{I}}
be a vector.
Then,
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is focused over
\bar{O}\setminus\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
and
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is not focused over any larger subset of the non-outputs.

Proof.

Let
F_{\vec{\mathscr{a}}}=\bar{O}\setminus\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
.
By inspecting conditions
(Fs1)
and
(Fs2)
, for any sets
S,S^{\prime}\subseteq\bar{O}
, the vector
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is simultaneously focused over
S
and focused over
S^{\prime}
if and only if it is focused over
S\cup S^{\prime}
.
Therefore, it is sufficient to show that for all
v\in\bar{O}
:
v\in F_{\vec{\mathscr{a}}}
if and only if
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is focused over
\{v\}
.

(\Rightarrow)
: Let
v\in F_{\vec{\mathscr{a}}}
.
Then
v\notin\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
, so
(M\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
.
We consider two cases depending on
\lambda_{X}(v)
, following a similar proof structure as in Lemma
C.10
:

1.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, then:

0=(M\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=\sum_{u\in\bar{I}}M_{v,u}\vec{\mathscr{a}}_{u}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}\sum_{u\in\bar{I}}A_{v,u}\vec{\mathscr{a}}_{u}=\lambda_{Z}(v)^{-1}(A\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}

Since
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, necessarily
\lambda_{Z}(v)\neq 0
.
This implies
(A\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
, i.e.
v\notin\support\left(A\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
.
By contraposition: if
v\in\support\left(A\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
, then
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, i.e.
(Fs2)
holds.

2.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
.
Since
M_{v,u}=0
for
v\neq u
, we have:

0=(M\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=\sum_{u\in\bar{I}}M_{v,u}\vec{\mathscr{a}}_{u}=\lambda_{X}(v)^{-1}\vec{\mathscr{a}}_{v}

Then as
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, we have
\vec{\mathscr{a}}_{v}=0
, i.e.
v\notin\support\left(\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
.
Again by contraposition: if
v\in\support\left(A\right)
, then
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, meaning
(Fs1)
holds.

Hence
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
satisfies conditions
(Fs1)
and
(Fs2)
over the set
\{v\}
as required.

(\Leftarrow)
: Let
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
be focused over
\{v\}
.
Once again, we consider two cases on
\lambda_{X}(v)
:

1.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, then by
(Fs2)
:
v\notin\support\left(A\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
, i.e.
(A\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
.
Following the same sequence of equalities as in the first case of the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof, we find
(M\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
.

2.

Suppose
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, then by
(Fs1)
:
v\notin\support\left(\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
, i.e.
(\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
.
Following the same sequence of equalities as in the second case of the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof, we again find
(M\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
.

Therefore,
(M\vec{\mathscr{a}})_{v}=0
, meaning
v\notin\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
, ergo
v\in F_{\vec{\mathscr{a}}}
, which ends the proof.
∎

Using the above lemma, we find that the kernel of the flow-demand matrix forms is exactly the set of focused vectors.

Proof of Theorem
4.8
.

By Lemma
C.14
, a vector
\vec{\mathscr{a}}\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{I}}
is focused over
\bar{O}\setminus\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
.
Now, suppose
\vec{\mathscr{a}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\Gamma}
.
Then
\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is focused over
\bar{O}
.
Hence
\bar{O}=\bar{O}\setminus\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)
.
But
M\vec{\mathscr{a}}
is a vector in
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{O}}
, thus
\support\left(M\vec{\mathscr{a}}\right)=\emptyset
, i.e.
\vec{\mathscr{a}}\in\ker M
.
All these steps are reversible, which leads to
\mathfrak{F}_{\Gamma}\cong\ker M_{\Gamma}
.
∎

Appendix D
Finite field arithmetic

In finite fields, the complexity of arithmetic operations (by which we mean addition, negation, multiplication, and inversion) depends on the order
d
of the field
[
19
]
.
In main part of the paper, we consider
d
to be fixed (or at least bounded above), i.e. we treat the cost of arithmetic operations in
\mathbb{F}_{d}
as
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
In general, the cost of such operations can be expressed as
\Theta(\poly(\log d))
, i.e. it is polynomial in
\log d
.
The representation of an element of a finite field is possible in space
\Theta(\log d)
.
Addition and negation take
\Theta(\log d)
time, but the cost for multiplication and inversion is slightly higher.
It is known that
\mathcal{O}(\log d\log\log d)
time suffices for multiplication and
\mathcal{O}(\log d\log\log^{2}d)
for inversion
[
19
,
21
,
22
]
.
The result from
[
22
]
is critical here and is conditional on a widely believed hypothesis regarding prime numbers; this requirement may be omitted for fields with
d=p^{\ell}
where
\ell
is small (and
p
is prime)
[
41
, Theorem 3.35 and Theorem 3.41]
).
In particular, the stated complexity holds unconditionally for prime fields.
However, the methods with the best known complexity bounds are impractical for small
d
.
Yet again, for bounded
d
, we can treat these costs as
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

The exact complexity results, i.e. without fixing
d
, can be derived from the ones stated in the main part of the paper by including the cost of arithmetic operations.
For the time complexity, that means increasing lower bounds by
\log d
factors, and increasing upper bounds by
\mathcal{O}(\log d\log\log^{2}d)
.
The memory complexity increases by
\Theta(\log d)
, e.g. our
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding method requires
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\log d)
memory.

Appendix E
Pseudocode

\fname@algorithm

1
Construction of flow and order-demand matrices

1:

Returns the flow-demand matrix of a given labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph

2:

procedure

Flow-DemandMatrix
(
G,I,O,\lambda
)

3:

initialise
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
matrix
M

4:

for

v\in\bar{O}

5:

for

u\in\bar{I}

\triangleright
Loop over all entries of
M

6:

if

\ \lambda_{X}(v)=0

7:

M_{v,u}=\lambda(Z)^{-1}G_{v,u}

8:

else

9:

M_{v,u}=\lambda(X)^{-1}\delta_{v,u}

10:

return

M

11:

Returns the order-demand matrix of a given labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph

12:

procedure

Order-DemandMatrix
(
G,I,O,\lambda
)

13:

initialise
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
matrix
N

14:

for

v\in\bar{O}

15:

for

u\in\bar{I}

\triangleright
Loop over all entries of
N

16:

N_{v,u}=\lambda_{X}(v)G_{v,u}-\lambda_{Z}(v)\delta_{v,u}

17:

return

M

The pseudocode for the general
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding algorithm (which works even if
\left|I\right|\neq\left|O\right|
) then differs from the corresponding qubit algorithm in
[
31
, Appendix B]
on only a few lines:

•

On line
27
, we must look for non-zero values rather than values exactly equal to
1
. (In the
d=2
case, the only possible non-zero value is
1
.)

•

On lines
30
,
31
, and
37
, instead of simply adding a row to another row, we must add a row multiplied by some value such that the addition will cancel an undesired dependency in the second row. (In the
d=2
case, the multiplicity is always
1
.)

•

On a new line
38
, we must multiply the row by a suitable value so that its pivot becomes
1
. (In the
d=2
case, a pivot always necessarily equals
1
.)

Furthermore, while line
24
matches the previous (qubit) algorithm, we point out that the linear system comprises equations of the following form, where
val
corresponds to the value from the second block:

a_{1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{n_{O}-n_{I}}x_{n_{O}-n_{I}}+val=0

and not:

a_{1}x_{1}+\dots a_{n_{O}-n_{I}}x_{n_{O}-n_{I}}=val

It means, that the values encoded in the matrix
\mathcal{P}
(initialised on line
17
) are effectively negations of the values one would obtain if interpreting the system in the usual way. (When
d=2
, negation leaves elements invariant.)

\fname@algorithm

2
Finding flow in the general case

1:

Checks if a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow and, if it exists, returns the focused flow.

2:

procedure

FindFlowGeneral
(
G,I,O,\lambda
)

3:

M=\textsc{Flow-DemandMatrix}(G,I,O,\lambda)

4:

N=\textsc{Order-DemandMatrix}(G,I,O,\lambda)

5:

if

\rank M\neq n-n_{O}

6:

return
‘NO FLOW EXISTS’

7:

C_{0}=\text{any right inverse of $M$}

8:

F=\text{matrix with columns forming basis of $\ker M$}

9:

C^{\prime}=\left[C_{0}\mid F\right]

10:

{N}_{\mathcal{B}}=NC^{\prime}

11:

N_{L}=\text{first $n-n_{O}$ columns of ${N}_{\mathcal{B}}$}

12:

N_{R}=\text{last $n_{O}-n_{I}$ columns of ${N}_{\mathcal{B}}$}

13:

K_{ILS},K_{LS}=\left[N_{R}\mid N_{L}\mid Id_{\bar{O}}\right]

\triangleright
Two independent copies of the same linear system

14:

\triangleright
From now, we refer to the three parts of
K_{ILS}
and
K_{LS}
as the first block, the second block, and the third block

15:

bring first block of
K_{LS}
into row echelon form

16:

S=\emptyset

17:

initialize
(n_{O}-n_{I})\times(n-n_{O})
matrix
\mathcal{P}

\triangleright
Columns of
\mathcal{P}
correspond to non-outputs
\bar{O}

18:

while

S\neq\bar{O}
:

19:

find the first row
r_{z}
in
K_{LS}
whose first
n_{O}-n_{I}
entries equal
0

20:

L=\{v\in\bar{O}\setminus S\mid\text{column $v$ in the first $K_{LS}$ block has all entries from row $r_{z}$ on equal to $0$}\}

21:

if

L=\emptyset

22:

return
‘NO FLOW EXISTS’

23:

for

v\in L
:

24:

\mathcal{P}_{*,v}=\textsc{SolveLinearSystem}(\text{first block of $K_{LS}$, $v$ column in second block of $K_{LS}$})

25:

for

v\in L
:

26:

S=S\cup\left\{v\right\}

27:

R=\left[r\mid\text{$r$ is a row whose intersection with the $v$ column in the third block of $K_{LS}$ is $\neq 0$}\right]

28:

r_{last}=\text{last element of $R$}

29:

for

r\in R[:-1]

\triangleright
Iterate over all but last element

30:

add row
r_{last}
of
K_{LS}
with correct multiplicity to row
r
in
K_{LS}
to remove dependency

31:

add row
v
of
K_{ILS}
with correct multiplicity to row
r_{last}
in
K_{LS}
to remove dependency

32:

for

\ r\in\text{rows of $K_{LS}$ except $r_{last}$}
:

\triangleright
Iterate from top

33:

if

row
r
of
K_{LS}
has first
n_{O}-n_{I}
entries
0

34:

Break

35:

y=\text{column of the leading $1$ in row $r$ of $K_{LS}$}

36:

if

intersection of
r_{last}
and column
y
in
K_{LS}
is
\neq 0

37:

add row
r
with correct multiplicity to
r_{last}
in
K_{LS}
to remove dependency

38:

multiply
r_{last}
by a correct value that makes the first non-zero entry of
r_{last}
in the first block equal
1

39:

swap
r_{last}
with other rows to bring first block of
K_{LS}
back into row echelon form

40:

{C}^{\mathcal{B}}=\left[\frac{Id_{\bar{O}}}{\mathcal{P}}\right]

41:

return

\left(C^{\prime}{C}^{\mathcal{B}},NC^{\prime}{C}^{\mathcal{B}}\right)

Appendix F
Run of flow-finding algorithm

We now provide a detailed overview of how the pseudocode from Appendix
E
runs on the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Figure
1
.

Construction of the linear system

Firstly, in lines
3
and
4
, the flow-demand and order-demand matrices are created, these are the same matrices as presented in Figures
2(c)
and
2(d)
.
Since
\rank M=4
, the check in line
5
passes.
Next, in lines
7
,
8
, and
9
three further matrices are created: any right inverse
C^{\prime}
of
M
, a basis
F
of
\ker M
, and the concatenation
C_{0}
of these two.
For example:

C_{0}=[C^{\prime}\mid F]=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

where we use
F_{1}
as the label of the single column in
F
.
Next, in line
10
, we compute the product of
N
and
C^{\prime}
, denoted
N_{\mathcal{B}}
:

N_{\mathcal{B}}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

In lines
11
and
12
, we break
N_{\mathcal{B}}
into two submatrices
N_{L}
and
N_{R}
, and use them to obtain two (initially identical) copies
K_{LS}
and
K_{ILS}
of the following linear system.
We give primed labels to columns from the third block, to distinguish them from the corresponding columns in the second block:

K_{ILS}=K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

(4)

Next, in line
15
, we perform Gaussian elimination on the first block of
K_{LS}
, i.e. on the first column.
The independent copy
K_{ILS}
remains unchanged.
The Gaussian elimination involves adding the third row to the fourth row with multiplicity
2
so that the original pivot of the fourth block becomes
0
.
After that, we swap the first and third rows.
We drop the previous row labels, as they no longer make sense after performing row operations. (Nevertheless, the relationship of each modified row to the original vertex-labelled rows continues to be encoded in the third block.)
We obtain the following updated
K_{LS}
linear system:

K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

(5)

We initialise
S
and
\mathcal{P}
in lines
16
and
17
to:

\mathcal{P}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}\qquad\qquad S=\emptyset

First run of the main loop

We go into the main loop in line
18
.
During the first run, we identify that vertex
b
can be solved (line
20
).
This is because the entries of column
b
in the second block of the matrix in (
5
) are all
0
from the second row onwards.
Therefore
L=\left\{b\right\}
.
In line
24
, we identify the solution of the following linear system with a single equation (arising from the first block of
K_{LS}
together with column
b
), where we name the unique variable
f_{1}
:

1f_{1}+4=0

As the running example is over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
,
f_{1}=1
is the only solution:

\mathcal{P}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Now, we must transform the system of linear equations to the form it would have had if
b
had never been included (this is the loop starting in line
25
).
We add
b
to the set
S
of solved vertices in line
26
.
Next, we identify the set
R
of rows in the updated matrix that depend on the original row
b
of (
4
) by looking for non-zero values in column
b^{\prime}
of (
5
).
This yields
R=\left\{1,4\right\}
and
r_{last}=4
.

We enter the next for loop in line
29
: here, this loop runs only once, for
r=1
, i.e. the first row.
In line
31
, we add row
r_{last}
, i.e. the fourth row, to the first row to cancel dependency on original
b
row from the latter.
From column
b^{\prime}
, the first row depends on
1
times the original row
b
while the fourth row depends on
2
times the original row
b
.
Thus, we must add
2
times the fourth row to the first row to cancel the dependency, obtaining:

K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

(6)

We exit the loop that started on line
29
.
At this point, only the fourth row has any dependency on row
b
from initial system
K_{ILS}
.
More specifically, the dependency is on
2
times row
b
.
We remove this dependency in line
31
by subtracting 2 times the
b
row of
K_{ILS}
from the fourth row of the current
K_{LS}
given in (
6
).
This is equivalent to adding 3 times row
b
from
K_{ILS}
to row 4 of
K_{LS}
, since
-2=3
in
\mathbb{F}_{5}
; it yields:

K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

In the subsequent for loop from line
32
, we iterate over those rows of
K_{LS}
containing pivots, to cancel as much of fourth row as possible.
Here, this leads to the following system, obtained by adding 2 times the first row to the fourth row:

K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

(7)

In the second run of the loop from line
32
, we immediately break because the second row of
K_{LS}
has no pivot in the first block.
Finally, in lines
38
and
39
nothing happens, as the fourth row is already identically zero in the first block (or in fact in all blocks) and is correctly placed.
This completes the first run of the main loop from line
18
.

Second run of the main loop

In the second run of the main loop, we observe that vertices
a
and
d
can be solved, but not the vertex
i
.
This is because the columns
a
and
d
in the second block are identically zero from the second row onwards, but the same is not true for column
i
.
Hence,
L=\{a,d\}
.
We find and store the solutions for these vertices.
The linear system for
a
is:

1f_{1}+0=0

hence
f_{1}=0
is the only solution.
The linear system for
d
is:

1f_{1}+1=0

hence
f_{1}=-1=4
is the only solution.
Thus, at the start of line
25
, we have:

\mathcal{P}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Now, we have to remove the dependencies on the original rows
a
and
d
.
We start with
a
: here the situation is simple, as only the second row of (
7
) depends on
a
(as indicated by column
a^{\prime}
in the third block).
Thus, after the entire run of the loop starting on line
25
, with
v=a
, we obtain the updated system:

K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

In the next run of the loop starting on line
25
, we have
v=d
.
Here, again only one row, the first row, depends on the original row
d
and the dependency has multiplicity
2
.
After this run of the loop, we get the system:

K_{LS}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

In the process,
S
is updated to contain
b
,
a
, and
d
.
This ends the second run of the main loop from line
18
.

Third run of the main loop

In the third run of the loop starting on line
18
,
i
can now be solved.
The corresponding linear system is:

0f_{1}+0=0

which is singular and consistent.
It is satisfied by any value of
f_{1}
.
So, for example, we can choose the solution
f_{1}=0
, leading to:

\mathcal{P}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Vertex
i
is added to
S
, i.e. at this point
S=\{b,a,d,i\}=\bar{O}
.
Afterwards, the system is still updated to the form it would have had if
i
was never included, which here will result in a system that has
0
s everywhere.

Final steps

Since
S=\bar{O}
, no more runs of the loop from line
18
occur.
Instead, on line
40
, we construct
C^{\mathcal{B}}
:

C^{\mathcal{B}}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

and finally output
C^{\prime}C^{\mathcal{B}}
, which is the reduced correction matrix, and
NC^{\prime}C^{\mathcal{B}}
, which encodes the partial order:

C^{\prime}C^{\mathcal{B}}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}\qquad\qquad NC^{\prime}C^{\mathcal{B}}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Note that the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow found here differs from that presented in Figures
1
and
2
, which is fine as the example has more outputs than inputs and so it can have multiple focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flows.
If, during the third run of the main loop, we had chosen the solution for
i
to be
3
instead of
0
, then we would instead recover the solution in Figure
2
.

Lastly, we note that the solutions found each run of the main loop from line
18
form the ‘layers’ of the partial order.
For the worked example, these layers are thus
[b]
,
[a,d]
, and
[i]
.
The algorithm for
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding from
[
8
]
would produce the same layers.
However, our method also returns the induced partial order
\prec_{C}
corresponding to the correction matrix
C
, as encoded in the product
NC=NC^{\prime}C^{\mathcal{B}}
.
This order is the minimal one (cf. Lemma
C.6
).
In general, the partial order returned by Algoritm
E
could be less restrictive than an order found via layers
5
5

5

Nevertheless, for this particular example, the minimal order is precisely the same as the order implied by ordering via found layers, i.e.
i\prec a\prec b
and
i\prec d\prec b
.
.

Appendix G
Lower bound for the complexity of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding

The complexity bound of our flow-finding algorithm is
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
only when
|I|\neq|O|
.
For
|I|=|O|
, we invoke subroutines for the problem of matrix inversion and multiplication, which, at least in principle, can be solved with sub-cubic complexity.
Here, we show that, in fact, reducing the other way is also possible, thus tightly linking the complexity of flow-finding with the aforementioned fundamental problems of linear algebra.

Firstly, the Theorem
4.5
implies the following corollary, which is more suitable for the discussion of lower bounds:

Corollary G.1
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, let
M
be its flow-demand matrix, and let
N
be its order-demand matrix.
Then
\Gamma
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow if and only if there exists a reduced correction matrix
C
over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
such that:

•

C
has shape
\bar{I}\times\bar{O}
,

•

MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
, and

•

NC
is the adjacency matrix of a directed acyclic
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph.

The
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow on
\Gamma
implied by the existence of this matrix
C
is
(C,\prec_{c})
, where furthermore the following holds for any
v,w\in\bar{O}
:

•

v\triangleleft_{c}w\Leftrightarrow(NC)_{w,v}\neq 0
and

•

\prec_{c}
is the transitive closure of
\triangleleft_{c}
.

Proof.

By inspection, this follows from Theorem
4.5
and its supporting Lemmata
C.14
and
C.13
.
∎

We adapt the lower bound argument for Pauli flow on qubits
[
31
, Theorem 4.3]
to
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow:

Theorem G.2

(
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding lower bound)
.

The problem of finding
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is at least as computationally expensive as the problem of finding the inverse of a matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.

Proof.

Let
M
be an arbitrary
n\times n
matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
Define
\Gamma:=(G,I,O,\lambda)
as follows:

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\right)

\displaystyle O

\displaystyle=\left(o_{1},\dots,o_{n}\right)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\left(I\cup O\right)

\displaystyle G_{i_{k},o_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=M_{k,\ell}\quad\left(\forall k,\ell\in\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\lambda(i_{k})

\displaystyle=(0,1)\quad\left(\forall k\in\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}\right)

Then the flow-demand matrix
M_{\Gamma}
of
(G,I,O,\lambda)
is precisely equal to
M
and the order-demand matrix
N_{\Gamma}
is identically zero.
Thus, by Corollary
G.1
,
\Gamma
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow if and only if
M
is invertible.
If
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow exists, the correction matrix is given by the inverse of
M
.
∎

The above theorem leads to an
\Omega(n^{2})
lower bound for
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-finding.
While this bound is already implied by the size of the input, it is generally expected that matrix inversion and multiplication require
\omega(n^{2})
field operations; currently a formal proof of this is known only for matrices over
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}

[
13
, Section 28.2]
.

Appendix H
Details about
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving rewriting

This section goes into more detail about the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving rewrite rules and related procedures summarised in Section
6
.
In Subsection
H.1
, we first explain known results about which graph operations can be implemented using local Clifford unitaries.
We use these in Subsection
H.2
to formally define pivoting and show that it is
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving.
In Subsection
H.3
, we prove that
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow reversal is also
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving.
In Subsection
H.4
, we show how to remove and insert Z-like vertices in an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving way.
Finally, in Subsection
H.5
, we combine Z-like insertion with other Clifford-based transformations to sketch an algorithm that generates arbitrary instances of labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph s with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

H.1
Graph operations that can be implemented via local Clifford unitaries

Several relevant rewrite rules were already defined and proved flow-preserving by Booth
[
10
, Section 5.1]
.
These rules are based on the two different types of operations on an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph which translate to local Clifford equivalences of the corresponding qudit graph states
[
6
, Section D]
.
The first of these operations is
local scaling
, which multiplies the weights of all edges incident on a chosen vertex
w
by some non-zero constant
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
.
Denoting by
\_\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w
the local scaling operation just described, we have:

(G\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w)_{u,v}:=\begin{cases}\gamma G_{u,v}&\text{if }u=w\text{ or }v=w\\
G_{u,v}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\quad\text{and}\qquad\quad\left|G\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w\right\rangle=\mathcal{R}^{(\gamma^{-1})}_{w}\left|G\right\rangle,

(8)

where
\mathcal{R}^{(\alpha)}\left|k\right\rangle=\left|\alpha k\right\rangle
for any
\alpha,k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
and the subscript
w
indicates that this single-qudit Clifford unitary is applied to qudit
w

[
10
, Section 5.1.1]
.

The second operation is
local complementation
, which is also parametrised by a constant
6
6

6

Unlike for local scaling, we may allow
\gamma=0
in this case. This yields a trivial transformation that leaves the graph invariant.

\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
and changes the weights of all edges among the neighbours of some chosen vertex
w
in a more complicated way.
Denoting by
\_\overset{\gamma}{\star}w
the local complementation operation of weight
\gamma
, we have:

(G\overset{\gamma}{\star}w)_{u,v}=\begin{cases}G_{u,v}+\gamma G_{u,w}G_{w,v}&\text{if }u\neq v\\
G_{u,v}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(9)

Note that
G_{u,w}G_{w,v}=0
unless both
u
and
v
are neighbours of
w
, so it is only edges between two neighbours of
w
that change.

Let
\tau
be the
d
-th root of unity that satisfies
\tau^{2}=\omega
and
\tau^{d}=1
.
Denote by
R
a qudit phase gate
7
7

7

Note that this is not the usual qudit phase gate, which is generally defined as
S\left|k\right\rangle=\tau^{k(k-1)}=RZ^{-2^{-1}}
, where
2^{-1}
is the multiplicative inverse of
2
in
\mathbb{F}_{d}
.
satisfying
R\left|k\right\rangle=\tau^{k^{2}}\left|k\right\rangle
and by
\tilde{R}:=HRH^{-1}
an equivalent gate in the
X
-eigenbasis.
Then the corresponding relationship between graph states is
8
8

8

This relationship is not entirely consistent with that given in
[
10
, Section 5.1.2]
, yet the differences consist only of Pauli operations which can be pushed past the Paulis used to define measurement spaces and corrections up to some scalar factor. Hence we can nevertheless use results from the latter source about the changes of measurement planes and the preservation of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow during local complementations.

\left|G\overset{\gamma}{\star}w\right\rangle=\tilde{R}^{\gamma}_{w}\prod_{v\in V}R^{-\gamma G_{w,v}^{2}}_{v}\left|G\right\rangle

(10)

cf.
[
7
, Proposition 12 of arXiv version]
, see Observation
H.8
in Appendix
H.2
for the translation from the ZX-calculus.
Both of the above operations have been shown to preserve
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow according to Definition
2.7
, i.e. working with full (not reduced) correction matrices.
By Theorem
3.2
, this implies that focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is also preserved.
Yet the changes necessary to preserve focusing might be more complicated
9
9

9

C.f.
[
39
, Lemma D.15]
for the explicit expression of the focused flow after a local complementation in the qubit case.
and will not be worked out here.

Proposition H.1

(
[
10
, Proposition 5.5]
)
.

Suppose the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow given by
(C,\prec)
.
Then, for any
w\in V
and any non-zero
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{*}
, the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w,I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
with new measurement labels

\lambda^{\prime}(u):=\begin{cases}(\gamma^{-1}C_{w,w},\gamma(GC)_{w,w})&\text{if }u=w\\
(C_{u,u},(GC)_{u,u})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

has an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec)
defined as

C^{\prime}_{u,v}:=\begin{cases}\gamma^{-1}C_{w,v}&\text{if }u=w\\
C_{u,v}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proposition H.2

(
[
10
, Proposition 5.8]
)
.

Suppose the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow given by
(C,\prec)
.
Then, for any
w\in\bar{I}
and any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
(G\overset{\gamma}{\star}w,I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
with new measurement labels
10
10

10

Note there is a typo in the definition of the updated labels in
[
10
, Eq. (5.61)]
, this has been corrected here.

\lambda^{\prime}(u):=\begin{cases}(C_{w,w}-\gamma(GC)_{w,w},(GC)_{w,w})&\text{if }u=w\\
(C_{u,u},(GC)_{u,u}-\gamma G_{u,w}^{2}C_{u,u})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

has an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow
(C^{\prime},\prec)
defined as

C^{\prime}_{u,v}:=\begin{cases}C_{w,v}-\gamma(GC)_{w,v}&\text{if }u=w\\
C_{u,v}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Remark H.3
.

The above transformation of the flow is much simpler than that presented in the qubit local complementation proof for (extended) gflow
[
1
, Lemma 3.1]
.
It can in fact be adapted to give a simpler transformation for gflow, though again this transformation does not preserve focusing.

In the binary case, given an edge
ww^{\prime}
in a graph, the sequence of three local complementations alternating about the two endpoints of the edge (in either order),
G\wedge ww^{\prime}=((G\star w)\star w^{\prime})\star w=((G\star w^{\prime})\star w)\star w^{\prime}
, is known as a
pivot
or an
edge-local complementation
.
Pivots also give rise to flow-preserving transformations and they interact better with focused gflow and Pauli flow than individual local complementations
[
3
, Lemma 5.3]
.
Again in the qubit case, the associated graph states satisfy
\left|(G\star w)\star w^{\prime})\star w\right\rangle=H_{w}H_{w^{\prime}}\prod_{v\in N_{G}(w)\cap N_{G}(w^{\prime})}Z_{v}\left|G\right\rangle
, i.e. a pivot corresponds to applying Hadamard gates to the two endpoints of the edge and
Z
-gates to all joint neighbours of both endpoints.

Generalising the qubit case, a family of pivot operations on qutrits was defined in
[
42
, Theorem 3.5]
.
Another work presents a qudit generalisation of the pivot-and-delete operation, where the vertices
w
and
w^{\prime}
are deleted from the graph after the pivot
[
34
, Lemma 10]
.

Motivated by the elegance of the qubit pivot operation, and the usefulness of having a graph operation that corresponds to Hadamard gates on certain qudits, we generalise the pivot operation to qudits.

Proposition H.4

(Formal version of Proposition
6.1
)
.

Let
G
be an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph with vertices
V
, suppose
w,w^{\prime}\in V
satisfy
\epsilon:=G_{w,w^{\prime}}\neq 0
, and define
G\wedge ww^{\prime}
to be the graph on
V
with the following adjacency matrix:

(G\wedge ww^{\prime})_{u,v}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }u=v\\
-\epsilon&\text{if }\{u,v\}=\{w,w^{\prime}\}\\
G_{w^{\prime},v}&\text{if }u=w\text{ and }v\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}\\
G_{u,w^{\prime}}&\text{if }v=w\text{ and }u\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}\\
G_{w,v}&\text{if }u=w^{\prime}\text{ and }v\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}\\
G_{u,w}&\text{if }v=w^{\prime}\text{ and }u\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}\\
G_{u,v}-\epsilon^{-1}(G_{u,w^{\prime}}G_{w,v}+G_{u,w}G_{w^{\prime},v})&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

Then the associated qudit graph state satisfies:

\left|G\wedge ww^{\prime}\right\rangle=F^{(-\epsilon)}_{w}F^{(-\epsilon)}_{w^{\prime}}\prod_{v\in V}R_{v}^{2\epsilon^{-1}G_{v,w}G_{v,w^{\prime}}}\left|G\right\rangle

where
F^{(\gamma)}:=\sum_{j,k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\omega^{\gamma jk}\left|j\right\rangle\!\left\langle k\right|
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
, and
R=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\tau^{k^{2}}\left|k\right\rangle\!\left\langle k\right|
.

As proving this result using standard matrix notation showed itself to be onerous and error-prone, we prove the above proposition in Appendix
H.2
using the qudit stabiliser ZX-calculus of
[
34
]
, which is also introduced in that appendix.

Analogous to the qubit case, a pivot operation can be built up from three successive local complementations.
Yet for qudits, at least one local scaling operation is also needed to get a pivot operation that is symmetric under interchange of
w
and
w^{\prime}
.

Observation H.5
.

Let
G
be an
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph with vertices
V
, suppose
w,w^{\prime}\in V
satisfy
G_{w,w^{\prime}}\neq 0
, and define
\gamma:=G_{w,w^{\prime}}^{-1}
.
Then:

G\wedge ww^{\prime}=(((G\overset{\gamma}{\star}w)\overset{-\gamma}{\star}w^{\prime})\overset{\gamma}{\star}w)\overset{-1}{\circ}w=(((G\overset{\gamma}{\circ}w^{\prime})\overset{1}{\star}w)\overset{-1}{\star}w^{\prime})\overset{1}{\star}w)\overset{-1/\gamma}{\circ}w

and similarly with the roles of
w,w^{\prime}
swapped.

The proof is straightforward but sufficiently onerous that we leave it out.
Nevertheless, as a pivot corresponds to a sequence of local complementations and local scalings, it is immediate that it preserves the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

H.2
Proving the pivoting property using the qudit stabiliser ZX-calculus

The ZX-calculus is a graphical language for reasoning about quantum computations.
While the qubit version is the most developed, there is also a complete ZX-calculus for the stabiliser fragment of
d
-dimensional qudits for every prime
d
.
The case
d=2
was shown in
[
4
]
and odd primes were treated in
[
7
,
34
]
.
Since generalising the pivot rule from the qubit case to qudits using algebraic techniques requires long, detailed, but not very insightful manipulations, and there is already a ZX-calculus proof of the related pivot-and-delete rule (also called “pivot simplification”)
[
34
, Lemma 10]
, we prove a (non-deletion version) of the pivot rule using the qudit ZX-calculus.
We will introduce here only those aspects of the rules and interpretation that are immediately necessary for the proof.
A reader interested in more detail may find a full introduction in
[
34
]
.

The qudit stabiliser ZX-calculus is generated by three kinds of components: green
Z
-spiders and red
X
-spiders, which have a phase label in
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2}
and may have arbitrary number of inputs and output wires, as well as Hadamard gates, which always have one input and one output:

a,b
\vdots
\vdots
m
n

a,b
\vdots
\vdots
m
n

A phase
0,0
on a spider is usually left out.
ZX-diagrams may be composed sequentially by connecting the input wires of one diagram with the output wires of another, and they may be composed in parallel via vertical juxtaposition.
Recall that
\tau
denotes the
d
-th root of unity satisfying
\tau^{2}=\omega
, and that
\left|k:U\right\rangle
for some
k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
denotes the eigenstate of the unitary operator
U
with eigenvalue
\omega^{k}
.
Then the components are interpreted as follows
[
34
]
:

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}}\left|k,\ell:Z\right\rangle\!\left\langle\ell,k:Z\right|

Every ZX-calculus diagram can be treated as a labelled graph in the sense that, as long as the input and output wires of the entire diagram remain in the same order, only the connectivity of the components matters; this is made rigorous via category theory.

The following two syntactic sugars will be useful.
Firstly, denote by

-

the inverse of the Hadamard operation so that
=
-
=
-

Secondly, introduce the
H-box
: a Hadamard with a label
a\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
called its
weight
.
This represents
a
parallel Hadamard edges between a pair of
Z
-spiders:

Observation H.6
.

An
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph state
\left|G\right\rangle
(cf. Definition
2.4
) is represented in the ZX-calculus as follows:

•

For each graph vertex, there is a green spider of phase
(0,0)
which is connected to one output wire.

•

For each edge of weight
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, there is an H-box of weight
k
connecting the spiders corresponding to the two endpoints of the edge.

For example, the
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-weighted graph state underlying the running example of Figure
1(a)
corresponds to the following ZX-diagram:

To prove an analogous result for pivots, we will need the following rewrite rules of the qudit stabiliser ZX-calculus.

Proposition H.9

(
[
34
, Proposition 6]
)
.

H-boxes of weight 0 and 1 satisfy

0
=

and

1
=

.
Additionally, H-boxes compose as follows:
x
y
xy^{-1}
=
x
y
xy
-
=
x
y
x+y
=

where in the first equality
y
has to be non-zero, but otherwise
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
are arbitrary.

Proposition H.10

(
[
7
, Proposition 8 of arXiv version]
)
.

The inverse Hadamard is equal to the H-box of weight
-1
, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{*}
the H-boxes of weight
x
and weight
-x
are inverse to each other, and repeating a non-zero--weighted H-box three times yields its inverse:
11

Having introduced the results required to prove the pivoting transformation, we are now ready to begin.
We follow the proof of
[
34
, Lemma 10]
with the only difference being that we have dangling output wires (and trivial phases) on the two endpoints of the pivot edge.
Nevertheless, we reproduce the proof here as we are interested in the resulting local unitaries on the two endpoints of the pivot edge, which are not immediately obvious from the pivot-and-delete version of the proof.

Lemma H.15
.

The following qudit pivot rule holds:

where
\alpha_{m}=-2\epsilon^{-1}e_{m}f_{m}
and
w_{m,n}=-\epsilon^{-1}(e_{m}f_{n}+e_{n}f_{m})
for all

.

Proof.

This is analogous to the proof of
[
34
, Lemma 10]
, using our Lemma
H.14
instead of their Lemma 9.
∎

We can now show which local unitary connects two graph states related by a pivot operation.

Proof of Proposition
H.4
.

Express the graph states in the ZX-calculus as in Observation
H.6
and note that we may treat non-existent edges as H-boxes of weight 0 (cf. Proposition
H.9
).
Apply Lemma
H.15
(bending the inputs into outputs) and use the standard interpretation to find

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\omega^{\epsilon jk}\left|j\right\rangle\!\left\langle k\right|=F^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\tau^{\alpha_{v}k^{2}}\left|k\right\rangle\!\left\langle k\right|=R^{\alpha_{v}}

Note that
(F^{(\gamma)})^{-1}=F^{(-\gamma)}
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
.
Hence the desired result follows by moving the single-qudit operators to the other side of the equation, noting that we may allow
R
operators on
w
and
w^{\prime}
as
\alpha_{w}=\alpha_{w^{\prime}}=0
, and that the single-qudit operators all commute, meaning
\bigotimes
can be changed to
\prod
.
∎

H.3
Proving flow reversibility

Before proving Theorem
6.3
, we provide a build-up with various supporting observations, like we have done in our proof for the qubit case
[
31
, Subsection 3.3]
.

We start by naming relevant types of measurements:

Definition H.16
.

We distinguish two types of measurements denoted ‘X-like measurements’
\mathcal{X}
and ‘Z-like measurements’
\mathcal{Z}
as follows (as a reminder,
B
is the set of vertices internal vertices, cf. Definition
2.2
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in B\mid\lambda_{X}(v)=0\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in B\mid\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0\}

We exclude each vertex that is an input and an output simultaneously:

Observation H.17
.

Vertices that are both inputs and outputs do not impact the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow at all, neither in
\Gamma
nor in its reverse
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Thus, from now on, we assume
I\cap O=\emptyset
and
V=I\cup\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{Z}\cup O
.

Given a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph and some vertex
v\in\bar{O}
,
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow is preserved if
\lambda(v)
is replaced by its scalar multiple for any non-zero scalar in
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}

[
8
, Section 3]
.
For example, in the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Figure
1
, vertex
d
could instead be given the measurement label
3\cdot(4,2)=(2,1)
and this would preserve the existence of
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.

Now, for any measurement label
(a,b)
, there exists
c\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
such that
(ac,bc)
is of the form
(0,1)
or
(1,k)
for some
k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
: if
a=0
, then necessarily
b\neq 0
, so taking
c=b^{-1}
works; and if
a\neq 0
, taking
c=a^{-1}
works.
Therefore, without loss of generality, one may work with normalised labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph s in the following sense:

Definition H.18
.

A labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph is called
normalised
if all measurements labels are of the form
(0,1)
or
(1,k)
for some
k\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
.

For example, normalisation of the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Figure
1
results in the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph in Figure
3
.

2
4
4
3
1
4
i
a
o_{1}
b
d
o_{2}
(0,1)
(0,1)
(1,0)
(1,3)
3
2

Figure 3
:
The labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph from Figure
1
after normalisation of measurement labels.

After the normalisation of measurement labels, we will now introduce some notation related to the graph part of a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph.
Since we identify the graph with its adjacency matrix, let us name relevant submatrices of
G
as follows, where
*
stands for an unnamed submatrix:

G=:\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Now, we can rewrite the flow-demand matrix in block form:

Observation H.19
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a normalised labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph which satisfies
|I|=|O|
and
I\cap O=\emptyset
; and let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be its reverse (cf. Definition
6.2
).
Then the flow-demand matrix
M
of
\Gamma
and the flow-demand matrix
M^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
satisfy:

M=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}\quad\text{and}\quad M^{\prime}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}^{T}

Similarly, we can present the order-demand matrix in block form:

Observation H.20
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,I,O,\lambda)
be a normalised labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph which satisfies
|I|=|O|
and
I\cap O=\emptyset
; and let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be its reverse.
In the order-demand matrix
N
of
\Gamma
, rows corresponding to vertices in
I
and in
\mathcal{X}
are defined analogously; yet this results in the rows corresponding to vertices in
I
being identically zero.
We can write the order-demand matrix as a product
N=PS
, where
P
is a diagonal
\bar{O}\times\bar{O}
matrix identifying the internal vertices, i.e.:

P:=\Diag\left(1\text{ for }v\in B\text{ and }0\text{ for }v\in I\right)\quad\text{and}\quad S:=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

with
J_{vw}=\delta_{vw}
and
D
being a diagonal matrix expressing
\lambda_{Z}(v)
, i.e.
D=\Diag\left(\lambda_{Z}(v)\text{ for }v\in\mathcal{Z}\right)
.
From now on, we write
K_{11}:=G_{11}-D
.
Note that
K_{11}
is symmetric because
G_{11}
is a diagonal block of the (symmetric) adjacency matrix and
D
is diagonal.
Moreover, while
J
is square, it is not generally the identity matrix because the sets of row and column labels need not match.

Similarly to
N
, the order-demand matrix
N^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
can be written as a product
N^{\prime}=P^{\prime}S^{\prime}
where
P^{\prime}
is a diagonal
\bar{I}\times\bar{I}
matrix:

P^{\prime}:=\Diag\left(1\text{ for }v\in B\text{ and }0\text{ for }v\in O\right)\quad\text{and}\quad S^{\prime}:=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}^{T}

where the last equality follows from the symmetry of
K_{11}
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
6.3
:

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\Gamma
is normalised and that
I\cap O=\emptyset
.

(\Rightarrow):
assume
\Gamma
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
By Theorem
4.5
, there exists
C
such that
MC=\Id
and
NC
forms a DAG, where
M
and
N
are the flow-demand and order-demand matrices of
\Gamma
, respectively.
By Observation
H.19
:

M=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Since
|I|=|O|
,
M
is square and thus, by Corollary
4.6
,
C
is unique and
C=M^{-1}
.
Break
C
into blocks corresponding to the block structure of
M
:

C=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Then:

MC=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

Therefore
C_{10}=0
and
C_{11}=\Id
by inspection.
Using these, the top left block implies
\Id=G_{00}C_{00}
, i.e.
C_{00}=(G_{00})^{-1}
(note that
G_{00}
is square).
Similarly, the top right block becomes
0=G_{00}C_{01}+G_{01}
.
Hence
G_{00}C_{01}=-G_{01}
and finally
C_{01}=-(G_{00})^{-1}G_{01}=-C_{00}G_{01}
.
To summarize:

C=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

NC=PSC=P\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}=P\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

H.4
Proving that
Z
-like removal and insertion are
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

Let
M
and
N
be the flow-demand and order-demand matrices for
(G,I,O,\lambda)
respectively and let
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
be flow-demand and order-demand matrices for
(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
respectively.
Furthermore, let
\bar{O}^{\prime}
and
\bar{I}^{\prime}
be the set of non-output vertices and the set of non-input vertices of
(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
.

Let
C^{\prime}
be the correction matrix for
(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
, whose existence follows by Theorem
4.5
from the assumption that
\Gamma^{\prime}
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Thus
M^{\prime}C^{\prime}=\Id_{\bar{O}^{\prime}}^{\bar{O}^{\prime}}
and
N^{\prime}C^{\prime}
forms a DAG.
Since
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)\neq 0
, necessarily
v\notin I
and thus both a
v
-labelled row and a
v
-labelled column exist in each of
M^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime}
,
C^{\prime}
, and
N^{\prime}C^{\prime}
.

By construction (cf. Definitions
4.3
and
4.4
),
M
and
N
are submatrices of
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
.
Let
C
be the restriction of
C^{\prime}
to shape
\bar{O}\times\bar{I}
, the shape compatible with
M
and
N
.
We now show that
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
and that
NC
forms a DAG.

The
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow guaranteed by Theorem
4.5
is focused, so we can apply condition
(FZ)
to
C^{\prime}
to find
C^{\prime}_{v,w}=0
for any
w\in\bar{O}^{\prime}\setminus\{v\}
.
Therefore, for
u,w\in\bar{O}
:

(M^{\prime}C^{\prime})_{u,w}=\sum_{t\in\bar{I}^{\prime}}M^{\prime}_{u,t}C^{\prime}_{t,w}=\left(\sum_{t\in\bar{I}}M^{\prime}_{u,t}C^{\prime}_{t,w}\right)+M^{\prime}_{u,v}C^{\prime}_{v,w}=\left(\sum_{t\in\bar{I}}M_{u,t}C_{t,w}\right)+0=(MC)_{u,w}

Thus
MC
is the
\bar{O}\times\bar{O}
submatrix of
M^{\prime}C^{\prime}
, which implies
MC=\Id_{\bar{O}}^{\bar{O}}
.
By replacing
M^{\prime}
with
N^{\prime}
in the above equation,
NC
is the
\bar{O}\times\bar{O}
submatrix of
N^{\prime}C^{\prime}
; and since
N^{\prime}C^{\prime}
forms a DAG, so must
NC
.
Hence, by Theorem
4.5
,
(G,I,O,\lambda)
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.6
.

Let
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
be the flow-demand and order-demand matrices of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and analogously define
M
and
N
for
\Gamma
.
Suppose that
(G^{\prime},I,O,\lambda^{\prime})
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
Our goal is to find sufficient and necessary conditions that
\lambda^{\prime}
must satisfy and which can be checked in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.

Let
\bar{O}^{\prime},\bar{I}^{\prime}
be the non-outputs and non-inputs of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, respectively, i.e.
\bar{O}^{\prime}=\bar{O}\cup\{v\}
and analogously for
\bar{I}^{\prime}
.
By the constructions of flow-demand and order-demand matrices (i.e. Definitions
4.3
and
4.4
), we have:

M^{\prime}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad N^{\prime}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

where
\mathbf{0}
is identically zero.
The remaining three submatrices
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}\in(\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{O}})
and
\mathbf{n}\in(\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{I}})^{T}
are vectors defined as follows for any
u\in\bar{O}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{u}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lambda_{Z}(u)^{-1}S_{u}&\text{when}\ \lambda_{X}(u)=0\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{u}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{X}(u)S_{u}

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)(S\mid_{\bar{I}})^{T}

(11)

In particular,
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{z}
do not depend on
\lambda^{\prime}(v)
at all, while
\mathbf{n}
is scaled by
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)
but otherwise is also independent of
\lambda^{\prime}(v)
.

According to Theorem
4.5
, there exists
C^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}C^{\prime}=\Id_{\bar{O}^{\prime}}^{\bar{O}^{\prime}}
and
N^{\prime}C^{\prime}
forms a DAG.
Since
|I|=|O|
, by Corollary
4.6
, this

is unique.
Similarly,
C
is also unique, and by utilising the proof of Theorem
6.5
which describes the construction of a valid

as a submatrix of
C^{\prime}
, necessarily
C
forms a submatrix of
C^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, since Theorem
4.5
guarantees focused
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, by
(FZ)
we obtain
C^{\prime}_{v,u}=0
for
u\neq v
and by Definition
4.2
we obtain
C^{\prime}_{v,v}=\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)
.
Thus:

C^{\prime}=\scalebox{0.85}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\begin{pNiceArray}$}}

(12)

for some

.

H.5
Proofs relating to the generation of instances with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow

Usually, the term ‘flow-preserving rewriting’ refers to transformations that preserve both the linear operation implemented by a computation and the existence of flow.
Yet the existence of flow (or
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow) depends only on the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph, which does not contain enough information to determine a specific linear operator.
For example, it was pointed out in
[
1
]
that the deletion of a
YZ
-measurement is always flow-preserving, even though it preserves the interpretation only if the measurement angle is 0.
Thus, as argued in
[
5
]
, it can also be useful to analyse rewrite rules that preserve the existence of flow but not necessarily the interpretation.

We expect that the completeness proof from
[
5
]
can be adapted to the qudit setting, but leave that to future work, focusing solely on the generation of labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graphs with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow for now.
First, we prove a supporting lemma which shows that
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving rewriting can be used to transform any labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow into a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph without internal vertices.

Lemma H.22

(Deconstruction of a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow)
.

Given a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
\Gamma
with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow, a sequence of local-complementations and
Z
-like removals can bring
\Gamma
to a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with no internal vertices.

Proof.

Suppose
v\in B
.
If
\lambda_{X}(v)\neq 0
, then, by Theorem
6.5
,
v
can be removed while preserving
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
If instead
\lambda_{X}(v)=0
, then a local complementation about
v
(with any non-zero parameter
\gamma
) is flow-preserving and transforms
\lambda_{X}(v)
to some
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)
with
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}(v)\neq 0
, which again can be removed (cf. Proposition
H.2
).
Repeating this process for all
v\in B
while ignoring any Clifford effects on outputs eventually results in a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
B=\emptyset
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.7
.

Start by generating a random invertible matrix
M
over
\mathbb{F}_{d}
, for example by following
[
35
]
.
Construct the labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph
\Xi
with flow-demand matrix
M
via the construction from Theorem
G.2
.
By Theorem
4.5
,
\Xi
has
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow and
\Xi
is normalised (cf. Definition
H.18
).
Undo normalisation: for each input
i
, multiply
\lambda(i)
by some non-zero scalar from
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{*}
, transforming
\Xi
into
\Gamma^{(0)}
.
Now repeat the following
k
times:

•

Choose a random vector
S\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{V}}
and using Theorem
6.6
, attempt inserting a new
Z
-like internal vertex with connections determined by
S
, trying all measurement labels of the form
(a,b)\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
where
a\neq 0
.
If the insertion is not
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow-preserving for any measurement label, choose a different
S
and try again, otherwise pick any suitable label and move on to the second step.

•

Perform a local complementation around the newly inserted vertex with a randomly chosen (and not necessarily non-zero) parameter
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{d}
, ignoring any resulting local Clifford operators on outputs.

Finally, return the resulting labelled open graph
\Gamma^{(k)}
.

The above procedure always produces a labelled open
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-graph with
\mathbb{F}_{d}
-flow.
In particular, the procedure has a non-zero probability of reversing all steps from Lemma
H.22
when used on
\Gamma
.
Thus, with non-zero probability, the above procedure returns
\Gamma
.
∎

As of now, our method is not necessarily efficient: Choosing a random vector
S\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\bar{V}}
for connectivity of the new vertex may fail many times before finding a vector that does permit vertex insertion.
One can observe that an identically zero
S
always permits insertion, thus, with probability
1
, our algorithm eventually performs a vertex insertion (albeit potentially a trivial one).
However, bounding the efficiency of the insertion in a more meaningful way requires deeper analysis of the structure of all vectors
S
permitting insertions.
We leave this to future work.
